V
Outline of the remaining manuscript

The rest of the paper is devoted to technical derivations, but an attempt is made to make said derivations relatable.
In addition to providing nearly 50 example demonstrations of the various general ideas with specific molecules, we demonstrate our monodromy result using a simple toy model — the hydrogen molecule confined to rotate in two dimensions — in Sec.
VI
.

In Sec.
VII
, we review what is known about the position and momentum state space of an asymmetric molecule and calculate the holonomy of its position states.
In Sec.
VIII
, we formulate the momentum state space of symmetric molecules, proving the induced representation theorem stated above.
In Sec.
IX
, we formulate position states of symmetric molecules and the Fourier transform that converts between position and momentum.
In Sec.
X
, we calculate the holonomy of molecular position states, showing that it depends only on topological features of the position configuration space.
In Sec.
XI
, we quantify the strength of the fiber as a quantum error-correcting code.
In Sec.
XII
, we discuss how to realize the monodromy of homonuclear molecules via various interferometric approaches.

VI
Toy model: hydrogen molecule in two dimensions

We demonstrate a simple example of the molecules species classification and monodromy effects using diatomic molecules, pinned at their center of mass and confined to rotate in the
\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}
plane.
All rotations in this section are restricted to be around the
\mathbf{z}
axis.

An example of an asymmetric diatomic in two dimensions is
HCl
.
Its state space is that same as that of the planar rotor (
a.k.a.
a particle on a circle)
Albert et al. 2017
.
Rotor momentum states
|J\rangle
are labeled by an integer
J
; their sign determines whether the rotor is spinning clockwise or counterclockwise.
The rotor’s momentum operator
\hat{J}
satisfies
\hat{J}|J\rangle=J|J\rangle
.
Rotor position states,

|\phi\rangle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}}\sum_{J\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{i\phi J}|J\rangle\,,

(7)

are labeled an angle
\phi
, which determines how the rotor’s orientation
deviates from some reference orientation.

On the other hand, the state space of perrotationally symmetric molecules is a subspace of the composite space consisting of a rotational planar-rotor factor and a nuclear-spin factor.
The subspace is defined by a constraint on both factors that stems from symmetry and spin statistics.

For example, the two spin-half nuclei of
\text{H}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
are indistinguishable in the molecule’s frame,
meaning that the molecule is symmetric under molecule-based rotations by
\pi
.
A
\pi
-rotation in the molecule’s frame rotates the molecule while also exchanging
nuclei.
This operation corresponds to a
perrotation
,

\overleftarrow{X}_{\pi}=e^{i\pi\hat{J}}\otimes\textsc{swap}\quad\quad\text{(molecule-based rotation)},

(8)

where the operator on the first factor acts on the rotational state
space,
and the operator on the second nuclear-spin factor performs a swap
of the two spins.

The joint rotation-spin constraint is

\displaystyle\overleftarrow{X}_{\pi}\psi_{\textnormal{mol}}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},s_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle=\psi_{\textnormal{mol}}(\phi_{2},\phi_{1},s_{2},s_{1})

(9a)

\displaystyle=-\psi_{\textnormal{mol}}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},s_{1},s_{2})\,,

(9b)

for any admissible wavefunction
\psi_{\textnormal{mol}}
of the two nuclear
positions
\phi_{1,2}
and spin-functions
s_{1,2}
. The minus
sign, stemming from the spin statistics of the two fermionic hydrogen nuclei,
can come from the rotational or the nuclear-spin factor,
resulting in two molecular
species
— para and “
ortho” hydrogen.

Basis states of the para species consist of tensor products
of rotor states with
even
angular momentum and the unique
anti-symmetric nuclear-spin
singlet
state,
\left|\uparrow\downarrow\right\rangle-\left|\downarrow\uparrow\right\rangle
.
Basis states of the ortho species consist of tensor products
of states with
odd
angular momentum and any nuclear-spin
state in the span of the three symmetric
triplet
states,

\left|\uparrow\uparrow\right\rangle
,
\left|\downarrow\downarrow\right\rangle
,
and
\left|\uparrow\downarrow\right\rangle+\left|\downarrow\uparrow\right\rangle
.
The required spin-statistics phase comes from the nuclear-spin (rotational)
factor for the para (ortho) species.

The two species’ spaces can also be defined using rotor position states
|\phi\rangle
(
7
).
If one of the nuclei is at position
\phi_{1}=\phi
, we automatically
know the position of the other nucleus,
\phi_{2}=\phi+\pi
modulo
2\pi
, so position
states of this dumbell-like molecule are labeled by a single angle. The
\pi
-perrotation symmetry
implies that states
|\phi\rangle
have to be identified with their
antipodes,
\left|\phi+\pi\right\rangle
. Identification is done by superposing
the two states for each angle
\phi\in[0,\pi)
. There are two ways to do so — using
either a
+1
or a
-1
relative phase — corresponding to the position states of the para and ortho
species, respectively.

Omitting the accompanying nuclear-spin factors for brevity, admissible rotational states can be expressed
in two different ways for each species,

\displaystyle|\psi_{\text{para}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\text{ even}}c_{J}^{\text{para}}|J\rangle=\int_{0}^{\pi}\textnormal{d}\phi\,c^{\text{para}}(\phi)\left(|\phi\rangle+\left|\phi+\pi\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle|\psi_{\text{ortho}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\text{ odd}}c_{J}^{\text{ortho}}|J\rangle=\int_{0}^{\pi}\textnormal{d}\phi\,c^{\text{ortho}}(\phi)\left(|\phi\rangle-\left|\phi+\pi\right\rangle\right),

(10)

for some momentum-state coefficients
c_{J}
and position-state
functions
c(\phi)
.

Monodromy occurs when we examine how the two species’ states transform
under “passive” lab-based symmetry rotations.
Such rotations occur in the lab frame, there is no permutation action on the spins.
The
\pi
-rotation,

\overrightharpoon{X}_{\pi}=e^{i\pi\hat{J}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\quad\quad\text{(lab-based rotation)},

(11)

rotates each nucleus to a position that coincides with the initial
position of the other nucleus.

Lab-based rotations should, in some physical settings, be generated by the
total

angular momentum,
\hat{J}+\hat{s}_{1}+\hat{s}_{2}
,
where
\hat{s}_{1,2}
are momentum generators for the two spins.
These produce extra transversal operations that re-orient the individual nuclei to a new frame, but they cannot produce perrotational permutation factors like the
swap
operation in Eq. (
8
).
We ignore such effects in this work for
simplicity, noting that they may have to be taken into account in an experimental setting.

The hydrogen molecule is symmetric under the lab-based rotation from Eq. (
11
),
but the two species transform in different ways:

\displaystyle\overrightharpoon{X}_{\pi}|\psi_{\text{para}}\rangle

\displaystyle=+|\psi_{\text{para}}\rangle

(12a)

\displaystyle\overrightharpoon{X}_{\pi}|\psi_{\text{ortho}}\rangle

\displaystyle=-|\psi_{\text{ortho}}\rangle\,.

(12b)

This can be shown either by noting that the two species are spanned
by momentum states of different parity, or by directly shifting each position
state by
\pi
in Eq. (
10
).
In the case that the molecule is initialized in a position state, the different signs correspond to the monodromy of each species after an adiabatic path from
\phi
to
\phi+\pi
in position-state space.

The above two species can also be obtained by restricting the theorem
from Sec.
II
to two dimensions. The symmetry group
of
\text{H}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
is
\mathsf{C}_{2}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, a subgroup of
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}\cong\mathsf{SO(2)}
, the
two-dimensional proper-rotation group.
The symmetry group has two irreps, the trivial irrep
a
and the sign irrep
b
.
The even momentum states of the para species house
the induced representation
\textsc{a}\uparrow\mathsf{SO(2)}
of the group of lab-based rotations, while the odd-momentum
ortho species correspond to
\textsc{b}\uparrow\mathsf{SO(2)}
.

More generally, a
\mathsf{C}_{N}=\mathbb{Z}_{N}
-symmetric molecule confined to a plane admits one or more of the induced representations
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}\uparrow\mathsf{SO(2)}
, where
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
is some root-of-unity irrep of
\mathsf{C}_{N}
.
Each representation corresponds to a particular way to quantize a particle on a circle.
The planar rotor from Eq. (
7
)
corresponds to
\textsc{a}\uparrow\mathsf{SO(2)}
— the representation induced by the sole irrep of the trivial group.

The rest of the manuscript addresses the extra complexities of molecules that are free to rotate in
three dimensions
.
In that case, the rotational factors,
e^{i\pi\hat{J}}
, on the right-hand side of Eqs. (
8
) and (
11
) no longer coincide.
So while the full rotation group of an asymmetric molecule is
\mathsf{SO(2)}
in two dimensions, it is
\mathsf{SO(3)}\times\mathsf{SO(3)}
in three dimensions because lab- and molecule-based rotations correspond to independent operations even for rigid bodies with no spin.

Figure 6:

Examples of asymmetric molecules (a) CaSH, (b)
\text{SOCl}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, (c)
HDO
, and (d)
\text{C}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{59}}}\text{${}^{13}$C}
from Examples
1
-
4
.
Any proper rotation maps each molecule into a different position.

VII
Asymmetric molecules

A molecule is said to be symmetric under a coordinate transformation when the transformation maps the molecule to the same position that the molecule was in before the transformation was applied.
For example, the
HCl
molecule,
when aligned along some axis, is symmetric under rotations around that axis by any angle.
These rotations are examples of the symmetry transformations or
symmetries
of such a molecule.

Symmetries include rotations that reorient the molecule while keeping the origin of its coordinate frame fixed, or reflections through either planes defined in the frame or the origin itself.
In our symmetry-centric perspective aiming to reveal the
angular
Hilbert space available for particular symmetries (as opposed to the Hamiltonian-centric perspective that focuses on spectra and dynamics of concrete molecules),
we consider only symmetries that can be applied to a rigid molecule by an observer.
All admissible symmetries therefore lie in
\mathsf{SO(3)}
, the group of orientation-preserving or
proper
rotations in three dimensions.

We say that a molecule is
asymmetric
if it admits no symmetries other than the identity; see Fig.
6
for examples.
In other words, any nontrivial proper rotation maps the molecule to a position that is different from the molecule’s initial position.
From now on, the most abundant nuclear-spin isotope is assumed for each nucleus when no specific isotope is given.

Example 1

(calcium hydrosulfide)
.

Since we consider only proper-rotation symmetries, an asymmetric molecule can still be symmetric under improper rotations such as reflections through planes.
An example of this is the CaSH molecule from Fig.
6
(a). It is not linear
Jarman and Bernath 1993
and is symmetric under reflection through the plane defined by its three atoms.
However, it is not symmetric under any proper rotations, so it is considered an asymmetric molecule for our purposes.

Example 2

(methylamine)
.

The
\text{CH}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}\text{NH}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
molecule also has reflection symmetry, but no proper rotation leaves the molecule invariant.
While a proper rotation by
2\pi/3
around the axis defined by the
CN
bond does cyclically permute the three hydrogen nuclei attached to the carbon, this rotation simultaneously rotates the bent
\text{NH}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
end into a different position.
The final position state of the molecule as a whole is thus different from the initial state under such a rotation.
In fact, there are no proper rotations that permute any subset of nuclei while leaving the rest of the molecule invariant, making this molecule asymmetric.

Example 3

(semi water)
.

While water is symmetric under a proper rotation, substituting one of its identical hydrogen nuclei with deuterium yields semi-heavy water,
HDO
[see Fig.
6
(c)], which is asymmetric.
Substituting a different spinful isotope for the oxygen in water does not change its symmetry because the oxygen atom is not permuted with another oxygen by any symmetry transformation.

Example 4

(
\text{C}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{59}}}\text{${}^{13}$C}
doped fullerene)
.

Perhaps the most drastic example of the symmetry breaking
Harter 2006
happens when one of the carbons in
\text{C}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{60}}}
fullerene is substituted with carbon-13 [see Fig.
6
(d)].
Since the carbon-13 is in a different place after any of the original symmetry rotations, the initial icosahedral symmetry of the molecule, which constitutes 60 different proper rotations, breaks down to no symmetry at all.

VII.1
Asymmetric position states

When applied to a fixed reference position, each rotation
\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{SO(3)}
maps the molecule to a position distinct from the reference; otherwise, the molecule would be symmetric.
We assume there are no other possible transformations that the molecule can undergo, so proper rotations
\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{SO(3)}
can be used as labels for the distinct position states
|\mathsf{r}\rangle
of an asymmetric molecule
(
Ezra 1982
, Appx. 2)
(
Harter 1993
, Ch. 5)
.
These position states are identical to those of any asymmetric rigid body, e.g., an airplane
Lynch and Park 2017
.

Molecular position states are “orthonormal” in the same sense as the position states of a free particle, satisfying

\langle\mathsf{r}|\mathsf{r}^{\prime}\rangle=\delta^{\mathsf{SO(3)}}(\mathsf{r},\mathsf{r}^{\prime})\,,

(13)

where the Dirac
\delta
-function is infinite for rotations
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{r}^{\prime}
and zero otherwise.
This relation makes the position states not normalizable —
\langle\mathsf{r}|\mathsf{r}\rangle
is technically infinite — but approximate normalizable position states exist which are localized at
\mathsf{r}
and which qualitatively have the same features (see Sec.
X.4.4
).
We focus on the idealized non-normalizable case since normalization is not relevant to most of our analysis.

Position states are also complete, yielding a decomposition of the identity,
\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{rot}}
, on the rotational state space of an asymmetric molecule,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{rot}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{SO(3)}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{r}\,|\mathsf{r}\rangle\langle\mathsf{r}|\,,

(14)

where
\textnormal{d}\mathsf{r}
is the Haar measure on
\mathsf{SO(3)}
with
\int\textnormal{d}\mathsf{r}=8\pi^{2}
.

The molecule’s reference position, denoted by
|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{e}\rangle
with
\mathsf{e}
the identity rotation, defines a
lab frame
of reference.
Meanwhile, the orientation of the molecule relative to that lab frame defines a
molecule frame
(
a.k.a.
the Eckhart frame
Eckart 1935
;
Judd 1975
;
Louck and Galbraith 1976
), which is “bolted” to the molecule and not allowed to rotate without the molecule rotating in the same way.

The rotation
\mathsf{r}
labeling the molecule’s position characterizes the orientation of the molecule frame relative to the lab frame.
Each frame can be re-oriented, and both types of re-orientations can be interpreted as rotations performed on the molecule (see Fig.
7
).

Re-orienting the molecule frame corresponds to a rotation performed on the molecule around an axis defined in the molecule frame.
Such
molecule-based rotations
, denoted by
\overleftharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}
for any
\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{SO(3)}
, act by post-multiplying the rotation matrix labeling the molecular position,

\overleftharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}{}|\mathsf{r}\rangle=|\mathsf{r}\mathsf{g}^{-1}\rangle\,.

(15a)

Re-orienting the lab frame is done by
lab-based rotations
, denoted by
\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}
.
These act by pre-multiplication,

\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}|\mathsf{r}\rangle=|\mathsf{g}\mathsf{r}\rangle~.

(15b)

Since the only piece of information encoded in molecular position is the
relative
orientation of the molecule w.r.t. the lab frame, we can fix the lab frame in place and think of lab-based rotations as “active” rotations of the molecule around an axis defined in the lab frame.

Figure 7:

Position of an asymmetric molecule is characterized by the relative orientation of the molecule frame (
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}xyz
axes) w.r.t. the lab frame (XYZ axes).
Each frame can be re-oriented independently, yielding two types of rotations.
Molecule-based
and lab-based rotations
rotate around axes defined in the molecule and lab frames, respectively (cf.
(
Lynch and Park 2017
, Fig. 3.9)
).
The resulting position state after the two types of rotations is generally
not
the same
[see Eq. (
15
)], and the full group of asymmetric rigid-body rotations,
\mathsf{SO(3)}\times\mathsf{SO(3)}
, consists of products of the two rotation types.

When the molecule is initially in its reference position
\left|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{e}\right\rangle
, a lab-based rotation
\mathsf{g}
maps the molecule to the same position as does the molecule-based rotation
\mathsf{g}^{-1}
.
This equivalence
corresponds to

\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}|\mathsf{e}\rangle=\overleftharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}^{-1}}|\mathsf{e}\rangle\equiv|\mathsf{g}\rangle~,

(16)

where it is necessary to take the inverse of
\mathsf{g}
since the two types of rotations change the relative orientation between the two frames in opposite ways.

In general, lab- and molecule-based rotations map an initial position
\mathsf{r}
to two
different
positions,

\mathsf{g}\mathsf{r}\neq\mathsf{r}\mathsf{g}
, because the group
\mathsf{SO(3)}
is
non-Abelian
.
Since their actions on position states are not equivalent, the two types of rotations correspond to distinct and independent operators on the rotational state space.
The full group of asymmetric rigid-body rotations is
\mathsf{SO(3)}\times\mathsf{SO(3)}
, realized by products of the lab- and molecule-based rotations.

It is not possible to use one type of rotation to induce the effect of the other in a
state-independent
way.
To see this, let
\mathsf{r}
be the initial molecular position. Applying a lab-based rotation
\mathsf{g}
yields the final position
\mathsf{g}\mathsf{r}
.
Alternatively, one can apply a molecule-based rotation
\mathsf{h}
that yields the same final position,
\mathsf{g}\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{r}\mathsf{h}^{-1}
, by solving for
\mathsf{h}
.
However, the solution,
\mathsf{h}=\mathsf{r}^{-1}\mathsf{g}^{-1}\mathsf{r}
, depends on the initial state because proper rotations do not commute.

The lab frame and its corresponding
\mathsf{SO(3)}
factor break down when the molecule is placed in an environment that is less symmetric than free space because symmetry-related positions are no longer distinguishable; this occurs, e.g., when the molecule is placed in a crystal lattice
(
Harter 1993
, pg. 372)
.
The molecule frame and its corresponding
\mathsf{SO(3)}
factor break down when the molecule itself is symmetric under some proper rotations.
Maintaining a perfectly symmetric
external
environment, we demonstrate, in subsequent sections, several features imposed by a molecule’s
internal
symmetry.

VII.2
Asymmetric rotational states

Lab- and molecule-based rotations are each generated by their own angular momentum operators, with each set yielding a basis for a copy of the Lie algebra of
\mathsf{SO(3)}
.
Due to the equivalence from Eq. (
16
), these two sets are coupled in the sense that they share the same total integer angular momentum
J\geq 0
.

A molecule can
can admit different values of the total momentum’s projection onto the lab- and molecule-frame axes.
As a consequence of the Peter-Weyl theorem
Folland 2016
;
Carter et al. 1995
;
Arovas
, the identity on the rotational state space,
\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{rot}}
from Eq. (
14
), can alternatively be decomposed into a direct sum indexed by the total momentum
J
,

\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{rot}}=\bigoplus_{J\geq 0}\mathbbm{1}_{2J+1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{2J+1}~,

(17)

with blocks of dimension
(2J+1)^{2}
that further split into two tensor factors
spanned by states of quantized values of the momentum components into the lab- and molecule-frame axes, respectively.
Above,
\mathbbm{1}_{d}
for natural number
d
denotes the
d
-dimensional identity matrix.

The choice of basis for the two
\mathbbm{1}_{2J+1}
factors in Eq. (
17
) is arbitrary, but both are typically expressed in terms of
rotational states

|^{J}_{m},k\rangle
, where
m
and
k
denote the
\mathbf{z}
-axis projection of the momentum into the respective frames.
We relegate
m
to a subscript because we will mostly focus on
k
.
The decomposition (
17
) becomes

\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{rot}}=\sum_{J\geq 0}\sum_{|m|\leq J}\sum_{|k|\leq J}|_{m}^{J},k\rangle\langle_{m}^{J},k|~.

(18)

Lab-based (molecule-based) rotations act only on the first (second) factor in Eq. (
17
), decomposing in terms of rotational states as
Harter 1993
(
Albert et al. 2020
, footnote 145)

\displaystyle\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{J\geq 0}D^{J}(\mathsf{g})\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{2J+1}

(19a)

\displaystyle\overleftharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{J\geq 0}\mathbbm{1}_{2J+1}\otimes D^{J\star}(\mathsf{g})\,,

(19b)

where
D^{J}
is a matrix representing
\mathsf{SO(3)}
rotations.

Rotational states
|^{J}_{m},k\rangle
can be expressed as superpositions of position states
|\mathsf{r}\rangle
, and visa versa,

\displaystyle|_{m}^{J},k\rangle

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}}}}\int_{\mathsf{SO(3)}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{r}\,D_{mk}^{J\star}(\mathsf{r})|\mathsf{r}\rangle

(20a)

\displaystyle|\mathsf{r}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\geq 0}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}}}}\sum_{|m|,|k|\leq J}D_{mk}^{J}(\mathsf{r})|_{m}^{J},k\rangle\,.

(20b)

The overlap between elements of these Fourier-dual angular position and momentum states,

\langle_{m}^{J},k|\mathsf{r}\rangle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}}}}D_{mk}^{J}(\mathsf{r})={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}}}}\langle m|D^{J}(\mathsf{r})|k\rangle\,,

(21)

is proportional to a Wigner
D
-matrix element, i.e., a matrix element of the rotation matrix
D^{J}
in the
\mathbf{z}
-axis momentum component eigenbasis
(
Varshalovich et al. 1988
, Ch. 4)
Zare 1991
.

The breaking down of the molecule frame due to symmetry results in
restricting the molecule-frame momentum space
\{|k\rangle\}
to particular subspaces.
We perform these restrictions in Sec.
VIII
, obtaining sets of momentum and position states for symmetric molecules.

VII.3
Incorporating nuclear spin

Asymmetric molecules have no symmetries, meaning that any rotations that permute nuclei will necessarily map the molecule to a distinct orientation (see Example
2
).
There are thus no restrictions on the state space due to spin statistics, so the nuclei are not required to be in any particular (e.g., single or triplet) state.
We denote the identity on the state space of any nuclear spins as
\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{nuc}}
.

The combined rotational-nuclear space for an asymmetric molecule is a tensor product of the rotational and nuclear-spin spaces,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{rot}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{nuc}}

(22a)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{SO(3)}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{r}\,|\mathsf{r}\rangle\langle\mathsf{r}|\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{nuc}}~,

(22b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\geq 0}\sum_{|m|\leq J}\sum_{|k|\leq J}|_{m}^{J},k\rangle\langle_{m}^{J},k|\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{nuc}}~,

(22c)

where we plug in the two position-state and rotational-state decompositions of the rotational factor from Eqs. (
14
) and (
18
), respectively.
If the nuclear spin of each atom participating in a given molecule is zero, then the
\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{nuc}}
factor is dropped, and only the rotational factor remains.

Given a basis
|\chi\rangle
for the nuclear-spin factor
\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{nuc}}
, a basis for the entire state space of an asymmetric molecule consists of states

|^{J}_{m},k\rangle_{\textnormal{rot}}\otimes|\chi\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}~,

(23)

where we have explicitly split each basis state into a factor coming from the rotational space and a factor coming from the nuclear-spin space.
Each of these states is rotation-spin
separable
, i.e., is in tensor-product form with respect to the rotation-spin factorization.
Rotation-spin non-separable or
entangled
states can then be expressed as superpositions of more than one of these states.

While there exist separable states in the state space of asymmetric molecules, the combination of symmetry and spin-statistics can make it impossible for separable states to exist at all in state spaces of symmetric molecular species.
In later sections, we show that, after imposing symmetry and spin-statistics constraints, rotation-spin basis states are dressed by the nuclear spin in a way that makes
each
basis state entangled [see Eq. (
57
)].
Since such states form an orthonormal basis, superposing them can only increase their degree of entanglement and thus cannot yield a separable state.

The nuclei carry angular momentum
S
in the form of nuclear spin, which can be defined with respect to the molecule frame (
\overrightharpoon{S}
) or the lab frame (
\overleftharpoon{S}
)
(
Rusconi and Romero-Isart 2016
, Table I)
.
When incorporating spin in certain physical settings, lab-based and molecule-based rotations are extended to rotate both the rotational degrees of freedom and the nuclear spins.
In other words, while the original rotations in Eq. (
19
),
\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}
and
\overleftharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}
, are generated by rotational angular momenta,
\overrightharpoon{J}
and
\overleftharpoon{J}
, composite rotations are generated by the total angular momenta,
\overrightharpoon{J}+\overrightharpoon{S}
and
\overleftharpoon{J}+\overleftharpoon{S}
, in the lab and molecule frames, respectively.
The extra nuclear-spin terms induce tensor-product rotations of the form
U^{\otimes M}
acting on any subset of
M
identical spins, where
U
is an
\mathsf{SU(2)}
rotation representing
\mathsf{g}
.
These operations are distinct from the nuclear-spin permutations highlighted in this work (due to Schur-Weyl duality
Harrow 2005
;
Schmiedt et al. 2016a
) and so are neglected from now on for simplicity, with the caveat that they may have to be accounted for in an experimental setting.

VII.4
Holonomy of asymmetric position states

We are interested in what happens when a molecule is initialized in a particular position state
|\mathsf{r}(0)\rangle
and then adiabatically traverses a
closed path
—
\{\mathsf{r}(t)\,,\,t\in[0,1]\}
with
\mathsf{r}(1)
identified with
\mathsf{r}(0)
— in its position space.
This path can, for example, be induced by applying lab-based rotations
\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{r}(t)}
, which rotate the molecule to a different position state according to Eq. (
15b
).

The full molecular position state in Eq. (
22b
) includes a nuclear-spin factor.
This factor tags along and is unaffected during the position-state path.
As such, we omit the nuclear states below for simplicity.

Upon traversing a closed path in its position-state space
\mathsf{SO(3)}
, the molecular state undergoes a
holonomy

Simon 1983
;
Zanardi and Rasetti 1999
;
Avron et al. 2012
,

|\mathsf{r}(0)\rangle\to U_{\textnormal{hol}}|\mathsf{r}(0)\rangle\,.

(24)

The holonomy operator is, in this case, a scalar whose argument is called the geometric or
Berry phase

Berry 1984
.
Note that a Hamiltonian is not required for determining the holonomy, which can be determined solely from the set of states and their parameter space.

The holonomy is invariant under changes in phase convention of the “instantaneous” states,
|\mathsf{r}(t)\rangle\to e^{i\varphi(t)}|\mathsf{r}(t)\rangle
,
and different phase conventions (sometimes called “gauges”) and path parameterizations provide different ways to calculate the same holonomy.

We parameterize closed paths by the same set of coordinates for all points except, potentially, the last point
\mathsf{r}(t=1)
.
To account for cases when the last point requires a different set of coordinates, we express
the holonomy as a product of two terms
(
Read 2009
, Eq. (1.2))
,

U_{\textnormal{hol}}=U_{\textnormal{mono}}\exp\left(-\int_{\mathsf{r}(0)}^{\mathsf{r}(1)}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{r}\,A(\mathsf{r})\right)\,.

(25)

The second term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
25
) is an integral of the
Berry connection
,

A(\mathsf{r})=i\langle\mathsf{r}|\partial\mathsf{r}\rangle/\langle\mathsf{r}|\mathsf{r}\rangle\,,

(26)

defined for all points
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{r}(t)
in the path but the last one (i.e., for
t<1
), and with the partial derivative being with respect to
t
.
This connection depends on “local” features of the parameter space, such as the curvature.
We will show that
A(\mathsf{r}(t))=0
for all
0\leq t<1
, which means the connection is
locally flat
.

The first term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
25
) — the
monodromy

U_{\textnormal{mono}}
— can occur when
\mathsf{r}(1)
is labeled with a
different
set of coordinates than
\mathsf{r}(0)
.
In order to resolve the labeling mismatch, the final state
|\mathsf{r}(1)\rangle
has to be re-expressed in terms of the initial state
|\mathsf{r}(0)\rangle
.
A non-trivial
U_{\textnormal{mono}}
is precisely the transformation relating the two states in the case when they are not equal; this happens when
\mathsf{r}(t)
parameterizes a non-contractible path.
As such, the monodromy piece depends on “global” or “topological” features of the parameter space.

The celebrated Aharonov-Bohm phase
Ehrenberg and Siday 1949
;
Aharonov and Bohm 1959
is due to a monodromy

Vasselli 2019
, and so is the
\pi
-phase due to circling a conical intersection
Mead 1992
.
In such Hamiltonian-based cases, a non-simply-connected parameter space is induced by the structure of the eigenstates.

Example 5

(conical intersection toy model)
.

Consider evolving a family of two-level system states
Alexandradinata
,

|\phi\rangle=\begin{pmatrix}\phantom{-}\sin\phi/2\\
-\cos\phi/2\end{pmatrix}\quad\quad\text{for}\quad\quad 0\leq\phi<2\pi~,

(27)

along the path
\phi(t)=2\pi t
. The Berry connection is locally flat for this case,
\langle\phi|\partial\phi\rangle=0
, a fact that we can immediately observe because all states in the path are real-valued
Simon 1983
. However, the state at the end point,
\phi(1)=2\pi
, still has to be re-expressed in terms of that at the initial point,
\phi(0)=0
, in order to conform to the chosen parameterization.
The two states differ by a phase,
\left|\phi=2\pi\right\rangle=-\left|\phi=0\right\rangle
, and re-expressing reveals a monodromy of
-1
.
This simple example holds more generally for any
2\times 2
symmetric matrix
Berry 1990
.

We now show that the Berry connection in Eq. (
26
) is locally flat for asymmetric molecular position states
|\mathsf{r}\rangle
.
Plugging in the expression in terms of rotational states in Eq. (
20b
), using
D_{mk}^{J\star}(\mathsf{r})=D_{km}^{J}(\mathsf{r}^{-1})
, and re-expressing the result as a trace of a product within the angular momentum space yields

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{r}|\partial\mathsf{r}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\geq 0}\sum_{|m|,|k|\leq J}{\textstyle\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}}}D_{mk}^{J\star}(\mathsf{r})\partial D_{mk}^{J}(\mathsf{r})

(28a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\geq 0}{\textstyle\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}}}\text{tr}\left(D^{J}(\mathsf{r}^{-1})\,\partial D^{J}(\mathsf{r})\right)\,.

(28b)

The quantity inside the trace is a representation the molecule’s angular velocity matrix relative to the molecule frame,
\mathsf{r}^{-1}\partial\mathsf{r}

(
Lynch and Park 2017
, Prop. 3.9)
.
Angular velocity is valued in the tangent space of the space
\mathsf{SO(3)}
of position-state labels, otherwise known as the
\mathsf{SO(3)}
or angular momentum Lie algebra
Varshalovich et al. 1988
.
This Lie algebra is generated by the angular momentum operators, all of which are traceless. Therefore, the angular velocity matrix is also traceless,
\text{tr}(D^{J}(\mathsf{r}^{-1})\,\partial D^{J}(\mathsf{r}))=0
for all
J
.
Since the numerator
\langle\mathsf{r}|\partial\mathsf{r}\rangle
is zero and the denominator
\langle\mathsf{r}|\mathsf{r}\rangle
is positive, the connection (
26
) is zero.

Since the space
\mathsf{SO(3)}
is not simply connected
Arovas
, there could be a nontrivial monodromy piece.
However,
U_{\textnormal{mono}}
turns out to be the identity for this particular case.
For example, if the original coordinates parameterizing position states are Euler angles
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
with
0\leq\alpha<2\pi
(
excluding

\pi
), but the path is parameterized by
(2\pi t,0,0)
(
including

\pi
), then the final point
(2\pi,0,0)
has to be mapped back into the initial point
(0,0,0)
.
The states corresponding to the two points are proportional (up to the monodromy) and, in this case, are exactly equal,

|\mathsf{r}=(2\pi,0,0)\rangle=|\mathsf{r}=(0,0,0)\rangle~,

(29)

yielding a
U_{\textnormal{mono}}
of identity.
We prove the general case using tools from the symmetric molecule formulation (see Example
37
).

Together, the above results show that there is no holonomy for closed loops in the position state space of an asymmetric molecule and, by extension, any asymmetric rigid body.
This should not be surprising since other “classical” states of fixed position do not have Berry phases either, such as position states of a free particle on a line or a planar rotor.
We will see in Sec.
X
that this is no longer the case for position states of many symmetric molecules with non-zero nuclear spin.

,

Table 1:

List of rigid-body symmetry groups, i.e., the possible subgroups of
\mathsf{SO(3)}
: the cyclic (
\mathsf{C}_{M}=\mathbb{Z}_{M}
for
M\geq 1
), dihedral (
\mathsf{D}_{M}
for
M\geq 2
), continuous (
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}\cong\mathsf{SO(2)}
,
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}\cong\mathsf{O(2)}
), and exceptional (
\mathsf{T}
,
\mathsf{O}
,
\mathsf{I}
) groups.
An example of a rotationally and perrotationally symmetric molecule is provided when possible.
Asymmetric molecules can be thought of as rotationally symmetric molecules whose symmetry group is the trivial group
\mathsf{C}_{1}
.
As such, symmetry alone is not sufficient to determine whether a molecule is rotationally or perrotationally symmetric: one needs to also know about the spin of any identical nuclei that are permutable via a symmetry rotation.
The last column lists group irreps, labeled by Mulliken symbols
Altmann and Herzig 1994
;
Ceulemans 2013
(see also
Mulliken 1933
;
Elcoro et al. 2017
).
We use complex (instead of real) irreps because we do not consider time-reversal symmetry.

VIII
Symmetric rotational states

In this work, we define a symmetric molecule as one whose position is invariant under a set of proper-rotation symmetry operations.
This set forms the molecule’s

\text{{rigid-body symmetry group},}~\mathsf{G}\subset\mathsf{SO(3)},

a subgroup of the group
\mathsf{SO(3)}
of proper (i.e, orientation-preserving) rotations.
This symmetry group is a subgroup of the
full
symmetry group of the “static molecular model”
(
Ezra 1982
, Eq. (2.21))
which can include reflections, improper rotations, and parity symmetry.
Since we concern ourselves only with symmetry transformations that are realizable by rigid-body rotations, we consider only
\mathsf{G}
and refer to it as simply “the symmetry group” throughout the text.

Subgroups of
\mathsf{SO(3)}
can be discrete, meaning that they have a finite set of elements, or continuous, meaning that some of their elements are parameterized by a continuous variable (see Table
1
).

The two continuous symmetry groups are isomorphic to the group of planar rotations,
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}\cong\mathsf{SO(2)}
, and the group of planar rotations and reflections,
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}\cong\mathsf{O(2)}
.
The
HCl
molecule, along with any heteronuclear diatomic, is invariant under rotations by any angle around its principal axis, meaning that its symmetry group is
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
.
The
\text{H}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
molecule, along with any homonuclear diatomic, is invariant under those same rotations and also
\pi
-rotations around axes perpendicular to its principal axis, all of which exchange its two constituent atoms; its symmetry group is
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
.

The possible discrete symmetry groups include the trivial group
\mathsf{C}_{1}
(corresponding to no symmetry at all),
the cyclic groups
\mathsf{C}_{N}
, and the dihedral groups
\mathsf{D}_{N}
(both for
N\geq 2
).
All asymmetric molecules, studied in Sec.
VII
, have the trivial symmetry group.
The
\mathsf{C}_{N}
group can be thought of as a discretized version of
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
, where rotations are only by multiples of
2\pi/N
.
Examples of
\mathsf{C}_{3}
-symmetric molecule is ammonia (
\text{NH}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
), whose atoms
do not
lie in a plane.
The
\mathsf{D}_{N}
group is a similarly discretized version of
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
.
Boron trifluoride (
\text{BF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
) or sulphur trioxide (
\text{SO}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
), whose four atoms
do
lie in a plane, both have
\mathsf{D}_{3}
symmetry.

Rounding out the list of symmetry groups are the three “exceptional” subgroups — the tetrahedral
\mathsf{T}
, octahedral
\mathsf{O}
, and icosahedral
\mathsf{I}
group.
Methane is a canonical example of tetrahedral symmetry — the symmetry group of a tetrahedron.
Examples of octahedral and icosahedral symmetry include sulfur hexafluoride (
\text{SF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{6}}}
) and fullere (
\text{C}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{60}}}
), respectively.
To reiterate, a (rigid-body) symmetry group consists of only proper rotations collected from the full symmetry group of a molecule, which is
\mathsf{O}_{\text{h}}
and
\mathsf{I}_{\text{h}}
, respectively, in the latter two cases.

There are cases where symmetry rotations permute identical nuclei,
and
those nuclei have non-zero nuclear spin.
A simple example is water, which admits a proper rotation that exchanges the two hydrogen atoms while leaving the oxygen atom intact.
In such cases, in addition to restricting the state space to only
\mathsf{G}
-symmetric states, we also have to take into account any Bose or Fermi spin statistics realized by the nuclei.

We split symmetric molecules into two categories, depending on whether or not symmetries permute identical spinful nuclei.
If all identical nuclei that are permuted under symmetry rotations have zero nuclear spin, then we call the molecule
rotationally symmetric
.
Identical nuclei in rotationally symmetric molecules can be treated as points, and such molecules are special cases of symmetric rigid bodies.
While technically correct, ascribing the invariance of such molecules to the statistics of any “spin-zero nuclei” is as necessary as attaching a zero spin to the end of a dumbell.

If any of the identical permuted nuclei have non-zero nuclear spin, then we call the molecule
perrotationally symmetric
.
Nuclei in perrotationally symmetric molecules have a non-trivial nuclear-spin space whose factors are permuted by molecule-based symmetry rotations, requiring a treatment deviating from that of symmetric rigid bodies.

For example,
dihydrogen
\text{H}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
is
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
-symmetric, but its two atoms have spin one-half nuclei.
A molecule-based rotation that exchanges the identical nuclei in
\text{H}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
results in a
-1
Fermi spin-statistics factor, making the molecule perrotationally symmetric.
On the other hand, disulfur
\text{S}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
is also
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
-symmetric, but has spinless nuclei.
There is no spin-statistics factor to worry about, so
\text{S}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
is rotationally symmetric and can be treated in the same way as a dumbbell.

A similar distinction is observed between rotationally
\mathsf{D}_{3}
-symmetric sulfur trioxide (
\text{SO}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
) and perrotationally
\mathsf{D}_{3}
-symmetric boron trifluoride (
\text{BF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
), and between rotationally
\mathsf{T}
-symmetric xenon tetroxide (
\text{XeO}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{4}}}
) and perrotationally
\mathsf{T}
-symmetric methane (
\text{CH}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{4}}}
).
More examples are provided in Table
1
.
To reiterate, shape and symmetry are not enough to determine whether a molecule is rotationally or perrotationally symmetric; one needs to also know about the spin of any identical nuclei.

VIII.1
Rotationally symmetric molecules

We now consider cases where molecule-based symmetry-group rotations
do not
permute identical spinful nuclei, meaning that the rotational state space of such symmetric molecules is identical to that of a rigid body with the same symmetry.

Rotationally
\mathsf{G}
-symmetric molecular states span a subspace of the asymmetric molecular state space, defined by
\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}
(
22
) and spanned by the basis in Eq. (
23
).
This subspace is defined by the restriction that

\overleftharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}|\psi_{\textnormal{mol}}\rangle=|\psi_{\textnormal{mol}}\rangle~,\quad\quad\forall\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}\,,

(30)

for any state
|\psi_{\textnormal{mol}}\rangle
of the symmetric molecule and any molecule-based rotation
\overleftharpoon{X}
.

In representation-theoretic terms, Eq. (
30
) implies that the molecule transforms under the trivial irreducible representation, or irrep, of
\mathsf{G}
.
In this irrep, denoted by
a
(and sometimes by
\textsc{a}_{1}
), all elements
\mathsf{g}
are mapped into one, i.e.,
\textsc{a}(\mathsf{g})=1
.
We need to keep only states in the decomposition of
\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}
(
22
) that transform as this irrep.
We can determine this space by evaluating molecule-based rotations at elements of the symmetry group, decomposing the result into all the irreps of
\mathsf{G}
, and keeping only copies of the trivial irrep.

Restricting
\mathsf{g}
to rotations in the symmetry group, each
D
-matrix for a given
J
, while forming an irreducible representating of
\mathsf{SO(3)}
, forms a
reducible
representation of
\mathsf{G}
.
This representation is unitarily equivalent to a block matrix whose blocks are labeled by any
\mathsf{G}
-irreps
\Gamma
that are present in the
D
-matrix.
Each block is characterized by two numbers, the dimension,
\dim\Gamma
, of the irrep and the number of copies of the irrep — the frequency or
multiplicity

\ml_{J}\Gamma
— present in the block.
The irrep dimension is
J
-independent, while the multiplicity can depend on the angular momentum.

We can “distribute out” the irrep and unitarily transform each block into tensor-product form, with the first factor housing the irrep, and the second factor housing the multiplicity space.
This yields the
isotypic decomposition
(
a.k.a.
canonical decomposition)
Harrow 2005
;
Folland 2016
,

D^{J\star}(\mathsf{g})\cong\bigoplus_{\Gamma\uparrow J}\Gamma(\mathsf{g})\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\ml_{J}\Gamma}\,,

(31)

where
\Gamma(\mathsf{g})
is the matrix representation of the group element
\mathsf{g}
, and
\mathbbm{1}_{\ml_{J}\Gamma}
is the identity matrix.
Not all
\mathsf{G}
-irreps participate in a given rotation matrix, and the shorthand notation
\Gamma\uparrow J
denotes the subset of irreps that are present in the above decomposition; such irreps are determined by “branching” or “subduction” rules.

Example 6

(
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
isotypic decomposition)
.

For example, rotation matrices
D^{J}
, when restricted to the group
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
of
\mathbf{z}
-axis rotations, are already diagonal w.r.t. the
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
-irrep basis,

D^{J\star}(\mathbf{z},\phi)=\sum_{|k|\leq J}e^{ik\phi}|k\rangle\langle k|~,

(32)

where
\phi
denotes the angle of the
\mathbf{z}
-axis rotation.
1
1

1

We abuse notation by substituting a rotation’s axis-angle
(\mathbf{v},\phi)
or Euler-angle
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
parameterizations
Chirikjian and Kyatkin 2000
for the rotation itself as the argument of the rotation matrix
D^{J}
.

The set of distinct
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
-irreps is in one-to-one correspondence with the integers
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}
(with
\lambda=0
the trivial irrep
a
), and each one-dimensional block houses the irrep
\Gamma_{\lambda=k}(\phi)=e^{ik\phi}
.
The set of participating irreps is thus

\Gamma\uparrow J=\{\Gamma_{\lambda=k}~\text{such that}~|k|\leq J\}~.

(33)

The multiplicity space is trivial in this case since each participating irrep is featured only once for each
J
.

Plugging the decomposition (
31
) into Eq. (
19b
) yields

\overleftharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}\cong\bigoplus_{J\geq 0}\mathbbm{1}_{2J+1}\otimes\bigoplus_{\Gamma\uparrow J}\Gamma(\mathsf{g})\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\ml_{J}\Gamma}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{nuc}}\,.

(34)

The next step is to apply the symmetry restriction (
30
),
which means keeping only the blocks corresponding to trivial irreps,
\Gamma=\textsc{a}
.
We do this with the help of a Kronecker
\delta
-function:
\delta_{\Gamma,\textsc{a}}=1
when
\Gamma=\textsc{a}
and zero otherwise.
The trivial irrep is one-dimensional,
\dim\textsc{a}=1
, so the first factor in the decomposition (
34
) reduces to a scalar factor,
\textsc{a}(\mathsf{g})=1
.
The second multiplicity factor remains nontrivial because each
J
can, in general, contain more than one copy of the trivial irrep.

All of the trivial-irrep blocks can be combined into a projection onto the state space of a
\mathsf{G}
-symmetric molecule,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}^{\mathsf{G}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{J\geq 0}\mathbbm{1}_{2J+1}\otimes\bigoplus_{\Gamma\uparrow J}\delta_{\Gamma,\textsc{a}}\mathbbm{1}_{\ml_{J}\textsc{a}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{nuc}}\,.

(35)

Expressing the first two factors in terms of a basis, Eq. (
35
) becomes

\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}^{\mathsf{G}}=\sum_{J\downarrow\textsc{a}}\sum_{|m|\leq J}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\ml_{J}\textsc{a}}|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle\langle_{m\kappa}^{J}|\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{nuc}}~,

(36)

where
m
and
\kappa
index the two factors for each
J
whose
D
-matrix contains
a
in its decomposition.
The sum over
J
becomes restricted to the subset of angular momenta which subduce to the trivial irrep. In (Frobenius
Folland 2016
) reciprocity to the notation used in Eq. (
31
), we denote this subset by the shorthand
J\downarrow\textsc{a}
.

The symmetric rotational states
|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle
are the angular momentum states of a
\mathsf{G}
-symmetric rigid body.
The key difference from the asymmetric case is the second factor in Eq. (
35
), spanned by the basis
\{|\kappa\rangle\}
of states that transform as the trivial
\mathsf{G}
-irrep.
Hamiltonian eigenstates and, more generally, any molecular states can be expressed as superpositions of the symmetric rotational states.

Figure 8:
Rotationally symmetric molecules (a)
HCl
, (b)
\text{S}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, (c)
\text{HCo}\text{(}\text{CO}\text{)}\text{}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{4}}}
, (d)
\text{SO}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
, and (e)
\text{C}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{60}}}
from Examples
8
-
12
.
Proper rotations in a symmetry group
\mathsf{G}
leave the positions of these molecules invariant.

\text{SO}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
(d) is shown in the reference position state chosen in Example
11
.

Example 7

(calcium monohydrosulfide)
.

The procedure for determining the state space of a symmetric molecule can also be applied to asymmetric molecules, such as the bent triatomic
CaSH

Sheridan et al. 2007
;
Augenbraun et al. 2020
[see Fig.
6
(a)].
The symmetry group of such molecules is trivial, containing only the identity element,
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{C}_{1}=\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle
.
This group has only one irrep, the trivial irrep
a
.
Each rotation matrix
D^{J}
, when restricted to
\mathsf{G}
, evaluates to the identity matrix whose dimension,
2J+1=\ml_{J}\textsc{a}
, indexes the multiplicity of the trivial irrep for that angular momentum.
We wind up with a re-expression of the asymmetric rotational states from Eq. (
22c
),
|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle=|_{m}^{J},k=\kappa-J-1\rangle
for all
J,m
and for
1\leq\kappa\leq 2J+1
.

Example 8

(hydrogen chloride)
.

Aligning the
HCl
molecule along the
\mathbf{z}
-axis, we observe that any rotation around the
\mathbf{z}
-axis leaves it invariant.
There are no other rotations that do this, so
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
.

Group elements
\mathsf{g}
of
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
are rotations by angle
\phi\in[-\pi,\pi)
, and their corresponding irreps,
e^{i\lambda\phi}
, are indexed by integers
\lambda
.
The case
\lambda=0
corresponds to the trivial irrep.

Recalling Example
6
, the
\mathbf{z}
-axis projection eigenstate state
|k\rangle
houses a copy of the
\lambda=k
irrep.
The trivial irrep is featured only once, at
k=0
, so there is no need for a
\kappa
index.
The
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
-symmetric rotational states are the subset of the asymmetric rotational states
\{|_{m}^{J},k\rangle\}
from Eq. (
22c
) with zero
k
,
|_{m}^{J}\rangle\equiv|_{m}^{J},k=0\rangle
for all
J,m
.
This basis corresponds to the spherical harmonics
Harter 1993
.

The states
\{|^{J}_{m}\rangle\}
span the rotational state space of any diatomic whose atoms are distinct, even in cases when its nuclei admit non-zero nuclear spin.
They also describe the state space of any
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
-symmetric linear polyatomic molecule, including
N
–
N
–
O
or
C
–
C
–
N
.

Example 9

(disulfur)
.

The
\text{S}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
molecule is a symmetric molecule with symmetry group
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
.
Aligning this dumbbell-like molecule along the
\mathbf{z}
axis, the rotations correspond to
\mathbf{z}
-axis rotations by arbitrary angles and form the subgroup
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
, while “reflections” correspond to
\pi
-rotations around any equatorial axis.
Symmetry-group rotations do exchange identical sulfur nuclei, but these nuclei do not have any nuclear spin, so this molecule is rotationally symmetric.

The
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
group admits a trivial irrep
\textsc{a}_{1}
, and a “sign” irrep
\textsc{a}_{2}
, which represents
\mathbf{z}
-axis rotations by
+1
and equatorial-axis rotations by
-1
.
The group also admits a countably infinite set of two-dimensional irreps, indexed by
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}
, for which
\mathbf{z}
-axis rotations by angle
\phi
are represented by
\exp(i\lambda\phi\sigma_{z})
, and the
\mathbf{y}
-axis “reflection” is represented by
\sigma_{x}
(given the usual Pauli matrices
\sigma_{x,z}
).

Since
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
is a subgroup of
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
, we can first apply the analysis of the previous example and consider which of the admissible states from that case,
\{|^{J}_{m},k=0\rangle\}
, transform as the trivial irrep of the larger group.

Since we have already determined how the
\mathbf{z}
-axis rotations act via Eq. (
32
), we are left to determine the remaining equatorial rotations.
Each such rotation is a product of a
\mathbf{z}
-axis rotation and a “fiducial”
\mathbf{y}
-axis
\pi
-rotation.
The latter is simply represented for each
J
,
1

D^{J\star}(\mathbf{y},\pi)=\sum_{|k|\leq J}\left(-1\right)^{J+k}\left|-k\right\rangle\langle k|~,

(37)

implying that the trivial irrep occurs only in
k=0
states with even momenta, with the remaining odd angular momentum states realizing the sign irrep.
The basis
\{|^{J}_{m},k=0\rangle\}
for even
J
corresponds to the even-momentum spherical harmonics.

The above basis spans the rotational state space of any
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
-symmetric linear molecule with spinless identical nuclei, e.g.,
C
–
N
–
C
or the closed-shell O
{}_{2}^{2-}
.

Example 10

(cobalt tetracarbonyl hydride)
.

The
\text{HCo}\text{(}\text{CO}\text{)}\text{}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{4}}}
molecule is symmetric under rotations by
\pm 2\pi/3
, which form the group
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{C}_{3}

Veillard et al. 1990
.
Symmetry-group rotations do permute identical C and O nuclei, but none of these nuclei have any nuclear spin, so this molecule is rotationally symmetric.

The group
\mathsf{C}_{3}
has three elements, denoted by angles
\phi\in\{0,2\pi/3,4\pi/3\}
,
and three irreps, with the trivial one denoted as
a
.
Picking the rotations to be around the (principal)
\mathbf{z}
-axis, we can utilize the decomposition from Eq. (
32
) and determine that the trivial irrep occurs whenever
k
is a multiple of three.
For example, when
J=4
, there are three states,
\left|\kappa=1\right\rangle\equiv\left|k=-3\right\rangle
,
\left|\kappa=2\right\rangle\equiv\left|k=0\right\rangle
, and
\left|\kappa=3\right\rangle\equiv\left|k=3\right\rangle
.

More generally, for
\mathsf{C}_{N}
-symmetric molecules that are aligned so that the symmetry group consists of
\mathbf{z}
-axis rotations, the basis
\{|^{J}_{m\kappa}\rangle\}
is a subset of the asymmetric rotational states
\{|_{m}^{J},k\rangle\}
from Eq. (
22c
) for which
k
is zero modulo
N
.
The basis states are

|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle\equiv\left|{}_{m}^{J},k=N(\kappa-1-n)\right\rangle

(38)

for all
J,m
, and for
\kappa
ranging from one to the multiplicity
\ml_{J}\textsc{a}=2n+1
, where
n=\lfloor J/N\rfloor
.

Example 11

(sulfur trioxide)
.

The symmetry group of the planar
\text{SO}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
molecule is
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}_{3}

Meyer et al. 1991
;
Maki et al. 2009
, the symmetry group of an equilateral triangle and the permutation group of the triangle’s three vertices.
This group is generated by two elements, the cyclic permutation
(\texttt{123})
, which maps vertex
\texttt{1}\to\texttt{2}
,
\texttt{2}\to\texttt{3}
, and
\texttt{3}\to\texttt{1}
, and the swap permutation
(\texttt{23})
, which swaps vertices
2
and
3
.
The permutation resulting from applying both of these generating permutations depends on the order in which the two are applied, making
\mathsf{D}_{3}
the smallest non-Abelian symmetry group.

Symmetry-group rotations leave
\text{SO}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
invariant while also permuting its three indistinguishable oxygen nuclei.
These nuclei can be thought of as the three vertices acted on by the group’s permutation representation.
Since the nuclei are spinless, we can treat the molecule as rotationally symmetric and project to the group’s trivial irrep
\textsc{a}_{1}
.

We place the molecule in the
\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}
-plane such that the cyclic nuclear permutation is performed by a
\mathbf{z}
-axis rotation by
2\pi/3
.
We further orient the molecule within the
\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}
-plane such that the swap of the 2nd and 3rd nuclei is performed by a
\mathbf{y}
-axis rotation by
\pi
[see Fig.
8
(d)].
Using the axis-angle representation of rotations
Chirikjian and Kyatkin 2000
, the two generating elements of the group correspond to
(\mathbf{z},2\pi/3)
and
(\mathbf{y},\pi)
, respectively.

The actions of the two generating rotations are described in Eqs. (
32
) and (
37
), respectively.
The asymmetric molecular states need to be superposed to yield states that transform according to the trivial irrep, yielding symmetric rotational states (cf. Wang functions
Wormer
;
Watson 1977
;
Papousek and Aliev 1982
)

|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\big(\left|{}_{m}^{J},k=3(\kappa-1)\right\rangle-\left(-1\right)^{J+\kappa}\left|{}_{m}^{J},k=3(1-\kappa)\right\rangle\big)~,

(39)

where
1\leq\kappa\leq\mathfrak{m}(J)
, and where the multiplicity is listed in
(
Altmann and Herzig 1994
, Table 23.10)
.
One can verify that
D^{J\star}(\mathbf{z},2\pi/3)|^{J}_{m\kappa}\rangle=D^{J\star}(\mathbf{y},\pi)|^{J}_{m\kappa}\rangle=|^{J}_{m\kappa}\rangle
, as desired.

Example 12

(C
60
fullerene)
.

The proper rotational symmetry group of the fullerene
Changala et al. 2019
;
Liu et al. 2022
;
Liu et al. 2023b
is the icosahedral group,
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{I}
. Carbon-12 has no nuclear spin, so the molecule is rotationally symmetric.

The trivial irrep of
\mathsf{I}
, denoted by
a
, is present only for certain angular momenta.
For example, the smallest nonzero momentum which houses this irrep is
J=6
, and the set of momenta that contain a trivial irrep, along with their multiplicities, are tabulated in
(
Ceulemans 2013
, Table 74.10)
.
Basis states
\{|\kappa\rangle\}
are not proportional to the
\mathbf{z}
-axis basis states
|k\rangle
, and their explicit expressions
Qiu and Ceulemans 2002
are not particularly illuminating.

Figure 9:

Perrotationally symmetric molecules (a)
\text{H}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}\text{O}
, (b)
\text{D}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, (c)
\text{NH}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
, (d)
\text{${}^{13}$C}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{60}}}
, (e)
\text{BF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
, and (f)
\text{CH}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{4}}}
from Examples
13
-
24
.
Proper rotations in a symmetry group
\mathsf{G}
leave the positions of these molecules invariant but can permute identical spinful nuclei.

VIII.2
Perrotationally symmetric molecules

Some symmetry transformations leave a molecule invariant while also permuting a set of indistinguishable molecular nuclei of nonzero spin
Wilson 1935a
;
Wilson 1935b
;
Longuet-Higgins 1963
;
Hougen 1971
.
A simple example is water, which admits a proper rotation that exchanges the two spin-half hydrogen atoms while leaving the oxygen atom intact.

In order to enforce the required nuclear-spin statistics,
the definition (
19b
) of molecule-frame rotations can be modified for the case of molecule-based symmetry-group rotations.
Each symmetry-group element corresponds to a rotation
\overleftharpoon{X}
(
19b
) tensored with a unitary matrix,
Perm
, that permutes the tensor factors in the nuclear spin space according to how the rotation permutes the nuclei.
We denote the full
perrotation
operation
Brester 1923
;
Wigner 1930
;
Gilles and Philippot 1972
(
Ezra 1982
, Eq. (2.25))
by a different overhead arrow,

\overleftarrow{X}_{\mathsf{g}}=\overleftharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}\otimes\texttt{Perm}(\mathsf{g})~,\quad\forall\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}~.

(40)

Lab-based symmetry-group rotations,
\overrightharpoon{X}
, are not appended with nuclear-spin permutations as the nuclei are merely moved from one position to another.

Not all molecule-frame symmetries permute identical spinful nuclei.
Those that do not have an identity permutation acting on the spin factor, and are still included in the symmetry group
\mathsf{G}
because they are necessary for enforcing rigid-body symmetry constraints.

Application of rotations
\mathsf{g}
that also permute nuclei needs to yield a
\pm 1
factor determined by the bosonic or fermionic spin statistics of permuted nuclei.
This sign is realized by a particular one-dimensional
\mathsf{G}
-irrep, which we call
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
.
Taken together, symmetry and spin-statistics require that

\overleftarrow{X}_{\mathsf{g}}|\psi_{\textnormal{mol}}\rangle=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}(\mathsf{g})|\psi_{\textnormal{mol}}\rangle~,\quad\quad\forall\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}\,.

(41)

The rotational state space of a symmetric molecule with nontrivial nuclear permutations is the subspace of the space defined by
\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{nuc}}
(
22
) that satisfies the restriction from Eq. (
41
).

In cases where no spinful nuclei are permuted by any symmetry-group rotations, the above reduces to the symmetry-only constraint in Eq. (
30
), for which
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
is the trivial irrep.
In the general case, our task is to find all states in the decomposition of
\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{nuc}}
that transform as the irrep
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
.
We do so by decomposing perrotations (
40
) into irreps of
\mathsf{G}
and keeping any copies of
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
.

The permutation piece “
Perm
” of the perrotations decomposes in the same way as each rotation matrix
D^{J}
does in Eq. (
31
).
We index the set of distinct
\mathsf{G}
-irreps present in the “
Perm
” representation of
\mathsf{G}
by
\Lambda
, with
\ml\Lambda
denoting the multiplicity of each irrep,

\texttt{Perm}(\mathsf{g})=\bigoplus_{\Lambda}\Lambda(\mathsf{g})\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\ml\Lambda}\,.

(42)

The nuclear-spin space is independent of angular momentum, so all above parameters are
J
-independent.

Plugging both the above
Perm
-matrix decomposition and the
\Gamma
-decomposition of the rotation
\overleftharpoon{X}
from Eq. (
34
) into the perrotation (
40
) yields

\overleftarrow{X}_{\mathsf{g}}=\bigoplus_{J\geq 0}\mathbbm{1}_{2J+1}\otimes\bigoplus_{\Gamma\uparrow J}\bigoplus_{\Lambda}[\Gamma\otimes\Lambda](\mathsf{g})\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\ml_{J}\Gamma}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\ml\Lambda}\,,

(43)

where we have collected the rotational and nuclear irrep spaces into
the collective representation
[\Gamma\otimes\Lambda](\mathsf{g})=\Gamma(\mathsf{g})\otimes\Lambda(\mathsf{g})
.
The joint symmetry and spin statistics restriction from Eq. (
41
) implies that we need to further decompose the tensor-product representation
\Gamma\otimes\Lambda
into irreps and keep any copies of
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
in that decomposition.

There are multiple ways to obtain
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
, each one falling out from the tensor product of a particular
\Gamma
and
\Lambda
.
Each distinct pair
(\Gamma=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}},\Lambda=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}})
of admissible irreps yields a subspace of rotational states called a
molecular species
, or species for short.
All admissible combinations can be extracted from direct-product tables
(
Ceulemans 2013
, Appx. E)
and are tabulated in Table
2
.

Given a symmetry group, there turn out to be only two possible values for
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
for each group, either trivial or not.
Additionally, fixing
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
and
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
uniquely determines
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
.
As such, each species
s
in Table
2
can be unambiguously and succinctly denoted by its rotational irrep only, either as
\textsc{s}=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
or as
\textsc{s}=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\ast}
.
The non-primed labels are for species for which
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
is trivial, while the labels with an asterisk are makr a nontrivial
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
.

From now on, we focus on a single species, noting that the full set of states of a given molecule corresponds to the span of all of the molecule’s species.

For each species
s
, corresponding to the irrep triple
[\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}\otimes\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}]\downarrow\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
, the dimensions of
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
and
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
turn out to be always the same.
We define the following shorthand,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}

\displaystyle\equiv\dim\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\dim\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}

(44a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}(J)

\displaystyle\equiv\ml_{J}\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}

(44b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}

\displaystyle\equiv\ml\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}\,.

(44c)

The identity on the state space of
s
decomposes in a similar way as the identity for a symmetric molecule in Eq. (
36
) in terms of momentum states
|^{J}_{m\kappa}\rangle
,

\textnormal{$\mathbbm{1}$}_{\textnormal{mol}}^{\mathsf{G},\textsc{s}}=\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}}\sum_{|m|\leq J}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle\langle_{m\kappa}^{J}|\otimes\sum_{\chi=1}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}}|\chi\rangle\langle\chi|~.

(45)

The indices
J
and
m
correspond to the standard lab-frame total angular momentum and its
\mathbf{z}
-axis
projection, respectively.
The first sum is only over those angular momenta which contain
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
in the decomposition of the rotation matrices
D^{J}
via Eq. (
31
); we denote this set by
J\downarrow\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
.
The third index
\kappa
labels the multiplicity space of
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
for each
J
.
The fourth
\chi
index labels the multiplicity subspace of
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
inside the nuclear-spin space, as only states in this subspace are allowed to accompany the species’ rotational states due to the spin-statistics requirement.
The multiplicity
\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}
is known as the statistical weight
Biedenharn et al. 2010
.

The key difference from the symmetric case is that the definition, and corresponding degree of rotation-spin entanglement, of each basis state
|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle
depends heavily on the dimension of the
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
irrep, specifically, on whether
\mathfrak{d}=1
or
\mathfrak{d}>1
.
