Table 2:
Table of all possible species of perrotationally symmetric molecular species.
Given a symmetry group, the species corresponding to the triple
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}\otimes\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}\downarrow\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
is unambiguously labeled by its rotational irrep (see Tab.
1
) for trivial
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
, with an “
\ast
” present in the superscript in case
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
is non-trivial.
The irrep dimension,
\mathfrak{d}\equiv\dim\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
, serves as the Schmidt rank, which quantifies the amount of entanglement of the species’ rotational basis states.

VIII.2.1
\mathfrak{d}=1
separable species

In this case, both irreps
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
and
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
are one-dimensional, and their tensor product directly
yields the correct spin statistics,
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}\otimes\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
.
To identify the state space, we can separately project the rotational and nuclear-spin space onto subspaces transforming as the two respective irreps.

Utilizing the Kronecker
\delta
-function from Eq. (
35
) for each space, we obtain

\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}^{\mathsf{G},\textsc{s}}=\bigoplus_{J\geq 0}\mathbbm{1}_{2J+1}\otimes\bigoplus_{\Gamma\uparrow J}\bigoplus_{\Lambda}\delta_{\Gamma,\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{m}(J)}\otimes\delta_{\Lambda,\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}}\,.

(46)

Since both irreps are one-dimensional, the two factors
\Gamma\otimes\Lambda
in Eq. (
43
) reduce to a scalar, leaving three remaining factors — the lab-frame
\mathbf{z}
-axis projection factor and the two multiplicity spaces — and thereby confirming Eq. (
45
).

In correspondence with the three factors in Eq. (
46
) and generalizing Eq. (
23
), the basis states are then

|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle_{\textnormal{rot}}|\chi\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}\,,

(47)

where we explicitly split each basis state into a factor coming from the rotational space and a factor coming from the nuclear space.
Each basis state is expressible in tensor-product form, so each basis state is separable w.r.t. the rotation-spin decomposition.
Possible entangled states include superpositions of such states for a given species or of states belonging to different species.

Example 13

(water,
\textsc{a}^{\ast}
para species)
.

Water is a symmetric molecule with proper-rotation symmetry group
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{C}_{2}
.
Its only element corresponds to a
\pi
-rotation around some axis
\bm{v}
such that the two hydrogen atoms and the oxygen are left intact.
This symmetry group has two irreps, the trivial irrep
a
and the sign irrep
b
, for which the rotation is represented by
-1
.

The molecule’s sole perrotation from Eq. (
40
) with a non-identity permutation component is
1

\overleftarrow{X}_{\bm{v},\pi}=\bigoplus_{J\geq 0}\mathbbm{1}_{2J+1}\otimes D^{J\star}(\bm{v},\pi)\otimes\textnormal{nuc}(\texttt{12})\,,

(48)

where
\texttt{Perm}(\bm{v},\pi)=\textnormal{nuc}(\texttt{12})
denotes the swap operation acting on the two nuclear-spin factors.

Since hydrogen nuclei are spin-
1/2
, i.e., fermionic, the nuclear exchange yields a
-1
sign.
Admissible molecular states should satisfy Eq. (
41
) with
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}=\textsc{b}
, the sign irrep of the symmetry group.
According to Table
2
, one way to achieve this is to pick
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\textsc{a}
and
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}=\textsc{b}
, corresponding to the
\textsc{a}^{\ast}
species of water.

Since the rotational side transforms as the trivial irrep, identifying the basis
\{|\kappa\rangle\}
is done in the same way as for symmetric molecules in Sec.
VIII.1
.
We align the molecule so that the symmetry-group rotation is around the
\mathbf{z}
-axis, which allows us to apply Eq. (
38
) for
N=2
.
In other words, the
\mathsf{C}_{2}
-symmetric rotational states are the subset of the asymmetric states
\{|_{m}^{J},k\rangle\}
for even
k
, with multiplicity
\mathfrak{m}(J)=2\lfloor J/2\rfloor+1
.

The nuclear-spin states
\{|\chi\rangle\}
that are paired up with the above rotational states span the multiplicity space of the
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}=\textsc{b}
irrep.
The
-1
factor upon exchange comes from this irrep, meaning that this space consists of all anti-symmetric states.

Each hydrogen nucleus is spanned by spin up and down states,
\left|\uparrow\right\rangle
and
\left|\downarrow\right\rangle
, respectively.
The composite four-dimensional space admits one anti-symmetric “singlet” state,
(\left|\uparrow\downarrow\right\rangle-\left|\downarrow\uparrow\right\rangle)/\sqrt{2}
, and there is no need for the index
\chi
since nuclear-spin multipicity
\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}=1
.
Species whose nuclei are in a singlet state are called para species.
The singlet state is tensored with an arbitary nuclear-spin state of the oxygen.

The fact that only some nuclear-spin states pair up with the trivial-irrep rotational states highlights a difference from the symmetric rigid-body case from Sec.
VIII.1
.
In that case, the overall irrep
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
is also the trivial irrep, but all nuclear states are allowed since symmetry rotations do not permute identical spinful nuclei.

Example 14

(water,
\textsc{b}^{\ast}
ortho species)
.

According to Table
2
, there is a second way to obtain the
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}=\textsc{b}
irrep for water, namely, by picking
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\textsc{b}
and
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}=\textsc{a}
.
This choice corresponds to the
\textsc{b}^{\ast}
species of water.

For this species, the rotational side transforms as the non-trivial irrep
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\textsc{b}
.
The
-1
factor upon exchange comes from this irrep.
Observing Eq. (
32
), this irrep occurs in all asymmetric states
\{|_{m}^{J},k\rangle\}
for which
k
is odd.
The lowest angular momentum of this species is thus
J=1
.

The hydrogen nuclear-spin states
\{|\chi\rangle\}
that are paired up with the above rotational states span the multiplicity space of
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}=\textsc{a}
, i.e., the trivial irrep.
This space, with multiplicity
\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}=3
, consists of all nuclear-spin triplet states and is spanned by
\left|\chi=1\right\rangle\equiv\left|\uparrow\uparrow\right\rangle
,
\left|\chi=2\right\rangle\equiv\left|\downarrow\downarrow\right\rangle
, and
\left|\chi=3\right\rangle\equiv(\left|\uparrow\downarrow\right\rangle+\left|\downarrow\uparrow\right\rangle)/\sqrt{2}
.
Species whose nuclei are in triplet states are called ortho species.
The triplet hydrogen-nuclei states are tensored with an arbitary nuclear-spin state of the oxygen.

Example 15

(deuterated hydrogen,
\textsc{a}_{1}
ortho species)
.

A simple species with bosonic nuclei is D
2
, where “D” stands for deuterium.
This is a symmetric molecule with the same symmetry group as disulfur from Example
9
,
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
.
However, the equatorial-axis rotations exchange the two spin-
1
deuterium nuclei, resulting in a
+1
spin-statistics factor that is realized by the trivial irrep.
Admissible molecular states should satisfy Eq. (
41
) with
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}=\textsc{a}_{1}
.
According to Table
2
, one way to achieve this is to pick
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}=\textsc{a}_{1}
, corresponding to the
\textsc{a}_{1}
species of D
2
.

Since the rotational side transforms as the trivial irrep, identifying the basis
\{|\kappa\rangle\}
is done in the same way as for symmetric molecules in Sec.
VIII.1
.
Per Example
9
,
asymmetric states
\{|_{m}^{J},k=0\rangle\}
for even
J
transform as the trivial irrep of
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
.

The nuclear-spin states also transform as the trivial irrep, and so are in the same triplet subspace as in Example
14
, with multiplicity
\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}=3
.

Example 16

(deuterated hydrogen,
\textsc{a}_{2}
para species)
.

The D
2
molecule admits another para species — the
\textsc{a}_{2}
species from Table
2
— which corresponds to picking the 1D irreps
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}=\textsc{a}_{2}
.
A basis for this species consists of tensor products of rotational states
|^{J}_{m},k=0\rangle
with
J
odd and singlet nuclear states (with multiplicity
\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}=1
).

None of the two-dimensional
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
-irreps feature in either the ortho or para species of this molecule.
This trend extends to all
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
-symmetric molecules, including ordinary hydrogen, because no combination of two-dimensional irreps yields the irreps
\textsc{a}_{1,2}
that describe the spin statistics.

In the case of ordinary
\text{H}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, the spin statistics are fermionic (
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}=\textsc{a}_{2}
), and there are two species —
\textsc{a}_{1}^{\ast}
and
\textsc{a}_{2}^{\ast}
.
These admit the same two respective sets of rotational states as the
\textsc{a}_{1}
and
\textsc{a}_{2}
deuterium species.
However, the ortho-para jargon is switched: the
\textsc{a}_{1}^{\ast}
admits a nuclear-spin singlet and so is called para-hydrogen, while the
\textsc{a}_{2}^{\ast}
hydrogen species is ortho.

Example 17

(ammonia,
{}^{2}\textsc{e}
species)
.

The
\text{NH}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
molecule is not planar, admitting the cyclic
\mathsf{C}_{3}
symmetry.
We ignore any tunneling effects for clarity
Bunker and Jensen 1998
,
noting that this example applies to any linear molecule that has been appended with three hydrogens in a
\mathsf{C}_{3}
symmetric way, e.g., calcium monomethoxide (
\text{CaOCH}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
)
Mitra et al. 2020
.

Rotations in this group are by angles
0
,
2\pi/3
, and
4\pi/3
, which wind up cyclically permuting the three hydrogen atoms.
This group has three irreps, the trivial one
a
, and two irreps
{}^{j=1,2}\textsc{e}
that are complex conjugate to each other and for which
{}^{j}\textsc{e}(2\pi/3)=\exp(i\frac{2\pi}{3}j)
.

A cyclic permutation of nuclei is equivalent to performing two exchanges, yielding a
+1
spin-statistics factor.
This corresponds to the trivial irrep
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}=\textsc{a}
.
According to Table
2
, one way to achieve this is to pick
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}={}^{2}\textsc{e}
and
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}={}^{1}\textsc{e}
, corresponding to the
{}^{2}\textsc{e}
species.

Aligning the molecule such that symmetry-group rotations are around the
\mathbf{z}
axis and observing Eq. (
32
), we see that the
|k\rangle
states of this species are all those for which
k\equiv 2
modulo 3.
The first momentum that harbors such states is
J=1
.
More generally, for
\mathsf{C}_{N}
-symmetric molecules, the momenta of the
{}^{2}\textsc{e}_{i}
species satisfy
J\geq i
.

Example 18

(boron trifluoride,
\textsc{a}_{2}^{\ast}
species)
.

The
\text{BF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
molecule has the same dihedral (
\mathsf{D}_{3}
) symmetry
Kuchitsu 1998
as sulfur trioxide from Example
11
.
Symmetry-group rotations leave both molecules invariant while also permuting their three indistinguishable nuclei.
In contrast to
\text{SO}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
, the fluorine nuclei of
\text{BF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
are spinful, making the latter molecule perrotationally symmetric.

The
\mathsf{D}_{3}
group — the permutation group of three objects — has three irreps.
The two one-dimensional irreps are the trivial irrep
\textsc{a}_{1}
and the “sign” irrep
\textsc{a}_{2}
, in which all two-object swaps are mapped to
-1
while remaining elements are mapped to
+1
.
The remaining two-dimensional irrep is called
e
, for which we let
\sigma=e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}}
and pick a basis
\{|\nu=1\rangle,|\nu=2\rangle\}
such that

\textsc{e}(\texttt{123})=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma&0\\
0&\sigma^{\star}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\textsc{e}(\texttt{23})=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(49)

The spin statistics of the spin-half flourine nuclei of
\text{BF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
require a
-1
to be produced every time a symmetry-group rotation swaps any two nuclei.
This corresponds to the sign
\mathsf{D}_{3}
-irrep, meaning that
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}=\textsc{a}_{2}
.
According to Table
2
, one way to achieve this is to pick
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\textsc{a}_{2}
and
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}=\textsc{a}_{1}
.
This corresponds to the
\textsc{a}_{2}^{\ast}
species of
\text{BF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
.

We orient the molecule within the
\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}
-plane in the same way as we did with
\text{SO}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
.
The cyclic nuclear permutation
(\texttt{123})
is performed by a
\mathbf{z}
-axis rotation by
2\pi/3
, with corresponding perrotation
1

\overleftarrow{X}_{\mathbf{z},2\pi/3}=\bigoplus_{J\geq 0}\mathbbm{1}_{2J+1}\otimes D^{J\star}(\mathbf{z},2\pi/3)\otimes\textnormal{nuc}(\texttt{123})\,,

(50)

where
\texttt{Perm}(\mathbf{z},2\pi/3)=\textnormal{nuc}(\texttt{123})
denotes the matrix that cyclically permutes the three nuclear-spin tensor factors.
The swap
(\texttt{23})
of the 2nd and 3rd nuclei is performed by a
\mathbf{y}
-axis rotation by
\pi
[see Fig.
9
(d)].

The rotations corresponding to the two generating permutations,
(\texttt{123})
and
(\texttt{23})
, act on the asymmetric rotational states (
22c
) as

\displaystyle D^{J\star}(\mathbf{z},2\pi/3)|_{m}^{J},k\rangle

\displaystyle=\sigma^{k}|_{m}^{J},k\rangle

(51a)

\displaystyle D^{J\star}(\mathbf{y},\pi)|_{m}^{J},k\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{J+k}|_{m}^{J},-k\rangle~,

(51b)

respectively.
The asymmetric molecular states
|k\rangle
need to be superposed to yield states that transform according to the sign irrep [cf. the trivial-irrep states in Eq. (
39
)].
The resulting rotational states of the species are

|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\big(\left|{}_{m}^{J},k=3(\kappa-1)\right\rangle+\left(-1\right)^{J+\kappa}\left|{}_{m}^{J},k=3(1-\kappa)\right\rangle\big)~,

(52)

where
1\leq\kappa\leq\mathfrak{m}(J)
, and where the multiplicity is listed in
(
Altmann and Herzig 1994
, Table 23.10)
.

The
2^{3}=8
-dimensional space of the three fluorine nuclei decomposes into four copies of the trivial irrep
\textsc{a}_{1}
, with the entire admissible subspace spanned by the states
\left|\uparrow\uparrow\uparrow\right\rangle
and
\left|\uparrow\downarrow\downarrow\right\rangle+\left|\downarrow\uparrow\uparrow\right\rangle+\left|\downarrow\downarrow\uparrow\right\rangle
, along with their two counterparts obtained by flipping all spins.
The quadruplet fluorine-nuclei states are then tensored with an arbitary nuclear-spin state of the boron to form the species’ nuclear-spin states.

Interestingly, the nuclear states do not decompose into any copies of the sign irrep
\textsc{a}_{2}
, thereby eliminating the possibility of the
\textsc{a}_{2}=\textsc{a}_{1}\otimes\textsc{a}_{2}
species for this molecule.
This is known as having “missing levels” in the spectroscopy literature
Harter and Patterson 1979b
;
Bunker and Jensen 1998
;
Bunker and Jensen 2004
.
Since the
\textsc{a}_{2}
species houses the
J=0
state, which transforms according to
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\textsc{a}_{1}
, symmetry and spin statistics make sure that
\text{BF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
never has zero angular momentum.

Example 19

(
13
\text{C}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{60}}}
fullerene,
a
species)
.

Fullerenes made up of carbon-12 have no nuclear spin and can thus be treated as rotationally symmetric molecules with icosahedral (
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{I}
) symmetry; we study this case in Example
12
.
On the other hand, isotopic
13
\text{C}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{60}}}
fullerenes are perrotationally symmetric since carbon-13 has a nuclear spin of
1/2
.

Each symmetry-group rotation acts in a way that permutes an even subset of the
60
carbon-13 nuclei, resulting in a
+1
spin-statistics factor that is realized by the trivial icosahedral irrep,
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}=\textsc{a}
.
According to Table
2
, one way to achieve this is to pick
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}=\textsc{a}
, corresponding to the
a
species.

The rotational states transform as the trivial irrep,
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\textsc{a}
, so identifying the basis
\{|\kappa\rangle\}
is done in the same way as in Example
12
.

The nuclear-spin space is restricted to only states
\{|\chi\rangle\}
that transform under the trivial irrep.
The full space is of dimension
2^{60}
, and finding a basis for the states is not the most tractable of tasks.
We can, however, numerically determine the nuclear multiplicity of
\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}=\numprint{19215358678900736}
(see Table
5
),
confirming earlier studies
Balasubramanian 1985
;
Harter and Reimer 1992a
;
Harter and Reimer 1992b
;
Balasubramanian 1992
;
Bunker and Jensen 1999
.

The other species of
13
\text{C}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{60}}}
use any of the four other
\mathsf{I}
-irreps,
\{\textsc{t}_{1},\textsc{t}_{2},\textsc{g},\textsc{h}\}
, for both
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
and
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
.
All such species have
\mathfrak{d}>1
.

VIII.2.2
\mathfrak{d}>1
entangled species

In general, the resulting tensor-product representation
\Gamma\otimes\Lambda
from Eq. (
43
) with
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
and
\Lambda=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
is of dimension
\mathfrak{d}^{2}
, per the notation defined in Eq. (
44
).
In cases where the dimension
\mathfrak{d}>1
, this tensor-product irrep has to be
restricted
to any copies of the one-dimensional
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
irrep that yield the correct spin statistics defined in Eq. (
41
).
For the
\mathsf{SU(2)}
Lie group, performing this restriction is analogous to coupling angular momentum vectors and then restricting to a particular total angular momentum sector
Georgi 2021
.

Let
\{|\nu\rangle_{\textnormal{rot}}\}_{\nu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}
and
\{|\nu\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}\}_{\nu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}
define bases for the irrep spaces of
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
and
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
, respectively.
The task is to find any states in the composite space, spanned by
\{|\nu\rangle_{\textnormal{rot}}|\nu^{\prime}\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}\}
, that transform according to the scalar irrep
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
.
In all cases (see Table
2
), there turns out to be only one such state,
which we denote by
|\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}\rangle
and which can be written as

\displaystyle|\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}\rangle

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}}}}\sum_{\nu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}s_{\nu}|\nu\rangle_{\textnormal{rot}}|\nu\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}\,,

(53)

whose Clebsch-Gordan coupling coefficients
s_{\nu}=\pm 1
depend on
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
,
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
, and
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
.

When
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
and
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
are trivial, we can prove the above result by writing down the projection onto the above state,

\varPi_{\textnormal{mol}}\equiv|\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}\rangle\langle\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}|~,

(54)

as a sum over symmetry group elements and applying Schur orthogonality
a.k.a.
the “Grand Orthogonality Theorem”
(
Arovas
, Sec. 2.4)
.
More generally, the above result can be obtained by consulting Clebsch-Gordan tables
(
Ceulemans 2013
, Appx. F)
.

Incorporating the above into the decomposition from Eq. (
43
), we first select the species corresponding to
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
and
\Lambda=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
and then project the tensor-product irrep into the above state,

[\Gamma\otimes\Lambda](\mathsf{g})\to\delta_{\Gamma,\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}}\delta_{\Lambda,\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}}\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}(\mathsf{g})\varPi_{\textnormal{mol}}~.

(55)

Backing out the identity on the resulting space, we have

\displaystyle\!\!\!\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}^{\mathsf{G},\textsc{s}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{J\geq 0}\mathbbm{1}_{2J+1}\otimes\bigoplus_{\Gamma\uparrow J}\bigoplus_{\Lambda}\varPi_{\textnormal{mol}}\otimes\delta_{\Gamma,\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{m}(J)}\otimes\delta_{\Lambda,\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}},

(56)

where we have split the rotational and nuclear-spin multiplicity factors and plugged in Eqs. (
44
).

Re-expressing the above identity factor as Eq. (
45
) yields basis states

|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle|\chi\rangle\equiv{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}}}}\sum_{\nu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}s_{\nu}|_{m\kappa}^{J},\nu\rangle_{\textnormal{rot}}|\nu,\chi\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}\,,

(57)

where we have incorporated the state
|\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}\rangle
(
53
) and explicitly split each state in the superposition on the right-hand side into a factor coming from the rotational space and a factor coming from the nuclear space.
This notation is different from that in Eq. (
3
), where we explicitly write the fixed entangled state; we continue to absorb it henceforth for notational simplicity.

When
\mathfrak{d}=1
, the internal
\nu
irrep index disappears, and Eq. (
57
) reduces to the separable case from Eq. (
47
).
When
\mathfrak{d}\geq 2
, the basis state for each
J
,
m
,
\kappa
, and
\chi
is a
completely
rotation-spin entangled state with
\mathfrak{d}
components, i.e., Schmidt rank
\mathfrak{d}

Ekert and Knight 1995
.
This entanglement is enforced by the combination of symmetry and spin statistics, manifest in the restriction from Eq. (
41
), and is impossible to separate without breaking the symmetry/spin-statistics requirement or transitioning to another species.
We can pick superpositions of the above states that yield a different basis with different labels, but there is no way to remove the sum over
\nu
in this way because it is its own separate factor.

Example 20

(boron trifluoride,
\textsc{e}^{\ast}
species)
.

Recalling Example
18
, we know that the spin statistics of the spin-
1/2
flourine nuclei of
\text{BF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
require a
-1
to be produced every time a symmetry-group rotation swaps any two nuclei.
This corresponds to the sign
\mathsf{D}_{3}
-irrep, meaning that
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}=\textsc{a}_{2}
.
According to Table
2
, the way to achieve this using multi-dimensional irreps is to pick
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}=\textsc{e}
and restrict their product to
\textsc{a}_{2}
.
This corresponds to the
\textsc{e}^{\ast}
entangled species of
\text{BF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
.

By comparing Eq. (
51
) to Eq. (
49
), we observe that a pair of states
\{|^{J}_{m},\pm|k|\rangle\}
form a basis for the
e
irrep whenever
|k|
is 1 modulo 3.
Taking care of our chosen order of the irrep basis, we also observe that
\{|^{J}_{m},\mp|k|\rangle\}
form a basis for the
e
irrep whenever
|k|
is 2 modulo 3.
The multiplicity
\mathfrak{m}(J)
for each angular momentum
J
is tabulated in
(
Altmann and Herzig 1994
, Table 23.10)
.

For example, for
|m|\leq J=1
, we have only
\mathfrak{m}(1)=1
copy of
e
, so there is no need for the multiplicity index
\kappa
.
We can then define the following basis,

\displaystyle\left|{}_{m}^{J=1},\nu=1\right\rangle_{\textnormal{rot}}

\displaystyle=|_{m}^{J=1},k=1\rangle

(58a)

\displaystyle\left|{}_{m}^{J=1},\nu=2\right\rangle_{\textnormal{rot}}

\displaystyle=|_{m}^{J=1},k=-1\rangle~,

(58b)

and verify that the two rotations from Eq. (
51
), when expressed in this basis, reduce to the two matrices from Eq. (
49
).

On the nuclear side, we omit the factor due to the boron spin for simplicity since that state is unrestricted by spin statistics.
The
2^{3}=8
dimensional spin space of the three spin-half flourine nuclei houses two copies of
e
, so the multiplicity
\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}=2
.
The first copy is spanned by the basis states

\displaystyle\left|\nu=1,\chi=1\right\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left|\uparrow\downarrow\downarrow\right\rangle+\sigma^{\star}\left|\downarrow\uparrow\downarrow\right\rangle+\sigma\left|\downarrow\downarrow\uparrow\right\rangle\right)/\sqrt{3}

\displaystyle\left|\nu=2,\chi=1\right\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left|\uparrow\downarrow\downarrow\right\rangle+\sigma\left|\downarrow\uparrow\downarrow\right\rangle+\sigma^{\star}\left|\downarrow\downarrow\uparrow\right\rangle\right)/\sqrt{3}~,

(59)

where one can verify that permuting the nuclei is equivalent to applying a corresponding product of powers of the matrices from Eq. (
49
).
The basis for the second copy,
\{\left|\nu,\chi=2\right\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}\}_{\nu=1,2}
, can be obtained from Eq. (
20
) by flipping all spins.

To construct the state
|\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}=\textsc{a}_{2}\rangle
from Eq. (
53
), we consult Clebsch-Gordan tables for
\mathsf{D}_{3}

(
Altmann and Herzig 1994
, 23.11, 3rd table)
.
This yields the entangled “rotation-spin singlet” state of Schmidt rank
2
,

|\textsc{a}_{2}\rangle=\left(|\nu=1\rangle_{\textnormal{rot}}|\nu=2\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}-|\nu=2\rangle_{\textnormal{rot}}|\nu=1\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}\right)/\sqrt{2}~.

(60)

Plugging this into Eq. (
57
) and using the bases for the “
rot
” and “
nuc
”
e
-irreps defined above yields the basis
\{|^{J}_{m\kappa},\chi\rangle\}
for this species.
For example,

|_{m}^{1},\chi\rangle=\left(\left|{}_{m}^{1},1\right\rangle_{\textnormal{rot}}\left|2,\chi\right\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}-\left|{}_{m}^{1},2\right\rangle_{\textnormal{rot}}\left|1,\chi\right\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}\right)/\sqrt{2}

(61)

for
|m|\leq J=1
,
\chi\in\{1,2\}
, and without a
\kappa
index since the multiplicity
\mathfrak{m}(J=1)=1
.
One can verify that these yield the correct spin statistics and transform as the
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}=\textsc{a}_{2}
irrep under symmetry-group perrotations, such as the one from Eq. (
50
).

Example 21

(methane,
t
species)
.

Methane
\text{CH}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{4}}}
has tetrahedral symmetry
\mathsf{T}

Herzberg 1966
;
Berger 1977
;
Hougen 2001
, with perrotations exchanging the four hydrogen atoms in the same way as the four corners of a tetrahedron are permuted by tetrahedral-group rotations.
Using
Table

2
, we see there are four species of
\mathsf{T}
:
a
,
{}^{1}\textsc{e}\phantom{{}_{i}}
,
{}^{2}\textsc{e}\phantom{{}_{i}}
, and
t
. Each of these is separable except for
t
, which is entangled with
\mathfrak{d}=3
.

The rotational state factor is not easy to track analytically, but they have been studied in related work by Harter and Patterson
Harter and Patterson 1977b
;
Harter and Patterson 1977a
;
Harter and Patterson 1977c
;
Harter and Patterson 1979b
;
Harter 1981
;
Harter 2006
.

The
2^{4}=16
dimensional spin space of the three spin-half hydrogen nuclei houses three copies of
t
, so the multiplicity
\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}=3
.
The first copy is spanned by the basis states

\displaystyle\left|\nu=1,\chi=1\right\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left|\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow\uparrow\right\rangle-\left|\uparrow\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow\right\rangle\right)/\sqrt{2}

(62a)

\displaystyle\left|\nu=2,\chi=1\right\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left|\downarrow\downarrow\uparrow\downarrow\right\rangle-\left|\uparrow\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow\right\rangle\right)/\sqrt{2}

(62b)

\displaystyle\left|\nu=3,\chi=1\right\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left|\downarrow\uparrow\downarrow\downarrow\right\rangle-\left|\uparrow\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow\right\rangle\right)/\sqrt{2}~.

(62c)

The second copy is spanned by the basis states

\displaystyle\left|\nu=1,\chi=2\right\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left|\downarrow\downarrow\uparrow\uparrow\right\rangle-\left|\uparrow\uparrow\downarrow\downarrow\right\rangle\right)/\sqrt{2}

(62d)

\displaystyle\left|\nu=2,\chi=2\right\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left|\downarrow\uparrow\downarrow\uparrow\right\rangle-\left|\uparrow\downarrow\uparrow\downarrow\right\rangle\right)/\sqrt{2}

(62e)

\displaystyle\left|\nu=3,\chi=2\right\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left|\downarrow\uparrow\uparrow\downarrow\right\rangle-\left|\uparrow\downarrow\downarrow\uparrow\right\rangle\right)/\sqrt{2}~.

(62f)

The third copy is spanned by the basis states

\displaystyle\left|\nu=1,\chi=3\right\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left|\downarrow\uparrow\uparrow\uparrow\right\rangle-\left|\uparrow\uparrow\uparrow\downarrow\right\rangle\right)/\sqrt{2}

(62g)

\displaystyle\left|\nu=2,\chi=3\right\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left|\uparrow\downarrow\uparrow\uparrow\right\rangle-\left|\uparrow\uparrow\uparrow\downarrow\right\rangle\right)/\sqrt{2}

(62h)

\displaystyle\left|\nu=3,\chi=3\right\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left|\uparrow\uparrow\downarrow\uparrow\right\rangle-\left|\uparrow\uparrow\uparrow\downarrow\right\rangle\right)/\sqrt{2}~.

(62i)

Notice that
\chi=i
corresponds to a superposition of those states with exactly
i

\uparrow
-spins and
(4-i)

\downarrow
-spins.

As with
\text{BF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
, admissible states include the factor
|\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}\rangle
, which consists of the above states summed over
\mu
for each
J
,
m
,
\kappa
, and
\chi
.
Exact expressions are no more illuminating than the general case.

VIII.2.3
Relative fraction of entanglement

A particular perrotationally
\mathsf{G}
-symmetric molecule admits a set
\{\textsc{s}\}
of species, each with dimension
\mathfrak{d}^{\textsc{s}}
, rotational multiplicities
\mathfrak{m}^{\textsc{s}}(J)
, and statistical weight
\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}^{\textsc{s}}
.
The identity of the entire molecular state space is a sum over the projections onto all of the molecule’s species,

\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}=\sum_{\textsc{s}}\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}^{\mathsf{G},\textsc{s}}~,

(63)

where each term on the right-hand side is determined by the prescription from earlier in this subsection.

Each species’ state space is infinite-dimensional.
However, by counting the total number of basis states for the entangled cases (
\mathfrak{d}^{\textsc{s}}>1
) and dividing by the total number of states, we can determine the
relative entangled-state fraction
,

\displaystyle F_{\textnormal{ent}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}}\sum_{\textsc{s}:\mathfrak{d}^{\textsc{s}}>1}\mathfrak{m}^{\textsc{s}}(J)\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}^{\textsc{s}}}{\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}}\sum_{\textsc{s}}\mathfrak{m}^{\textsc{s}}(J)\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}^{\textsc{s}}}

(64a)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{\textsc{s}:\mathfrak{d}^{\textsc{s}}>1}\mathfrak{d}^{\textsc{s}}\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}^{\textsc{s}}}{\sum_{\textsc{s}}\mathfrak{d}^{\textsc{s}}\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}^{\textsc{s}}}~.

(64b)

The second equality is the result of calculating the same relative fraction in position space (see Sec.
IX
), which allows us to obtain a closed-form expression.
The middle sum can be cut off at some momentum
J\leq J_{\max}
to obtain the relative fraction for a momentum-constrained subspace.

Example 22

(boron trifluoride)
.

Combining all species of
\text{BF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
and calculating the relative entangled-state fraction reveals that exactly half of all basis states are entangled.
This value, along with those of other molecules with dihedral perrotation symmetry, is listed in Table
3
.

Example 23

(methane)
.

Methane admits four species —
a
,
{}^{1}\textsc{e}
,
{}^{2}\textsc{e}
, and
t
— with only the last one being completely entangled.
We count the entangled-state fraction (
64
) of methane to be
F_{\textnormal{ent}}\approx 0.56
, meaning that just over half of methane’s rotational states are completely entangled.

(

\text{C}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{60}}}
fullerene)
.

The molecule
\hphantom{{}^{\text{13}}_{\text{}}}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}{}^{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\vphantom{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}\text{13}$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\vphantom{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}\text{13}$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\vphantom{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}\text{13}$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}\text{13}$}}}\kern 0.0pt\text{C}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{60}}}

\mathsf{I}
symmetry. From
Table

2
we see there are five species
a
,
\textsc{t}_{1}
,
\textsc{t}_{2}
,

, and

(which has
\mathfrak{d}=1
), with Schmidt ranks of

and

.

.
