IX
Symmetric position states

In this section, we formulate the state space of general rotationally
and perrotationally symmetric molecules in terms of position states,
culminating in Eqs. (
110
) and (
115
) for a general induced representation.
The formulation yields the same state space as that derived in Sec.
VIII
, and we develop the Fourier transform that relates position states of this section and momentum states from the previous section.

IX.1
Asymmetric molecules

The position state space of a symmetric molecule can be thought of as a subspace of the asymmetric molecular state space defined in Eq. (
22
).
For such purposes, it is more convenient for us to express the factor spanned
by the molecule-frame
\mathbf{z}
-axis projection states
|k\rangle

using a more general,
\mathsf{G}
-adapted basis
|\nu\kappa\rangle
stemming from
the isotypic decomposition from Eq. (
31
).
The basis change is

|_{m}^{J},k\rangle\quad\to\quad|_{m\kappa}^{J},\nu\rangle~,

(65)

where we group indices in a way that is consistent with the previous section.

Recalling Eq. (
31
), the
\mathsf{G}
-adapted basis
\{|\nu\kappa\rangle\}
block
diagonalizes the rotation matrices
D^{J}
, with each block corresponding
to a distinct
\mathsf{G}
-irrep
\Gamma
, and the entire set of participating
irreps denoted by
\Gamma\uparrow J
(see Example
6
). For each
\Gamma
,
the index
\nu
(along with Greek indices
\mu,\sigma
) labels the internal irrep space (and is removed when

\dim\Gamma=1
), and the index
\kappa
goes over the multiplicity space
(and is removed when
\ml_{J}\Gamma=1
). We suppress the
\Gamma
index
in the
\{|\nu\kappa\rangle\}
basis label set because, in all cases of
interest, we will be selecting a particular irrep — the trivial
irrep
a
for rotationally symmetric molecules, and a general
irrep
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
for perrotationally symmetric molecular species.

Indices of the original
|_{m}^{J},k\rangle
rotational states
are in one-to-one correspondence with the Wigner
D
-matrix elements

D_{mk}^{J}
(
21
), i.e., matrix elements of rotations
D^{J}

in the
\mathbf{z}
-axis basis. Generalizing the molecule-frame momentum factor, we define
the corresponding
\mathsf{G}
-adapted matrix elements
(cf.
(
Harter et al. 1978
, Eq. (2.33))
),

\langle_{m\kappa}^{J},\nu|\mathsf{r}\rangle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}}}}D_{m;\nu\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{r})={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}}}}\langle m|D^{J}(\mathsf{r})|\nu\kappa\rangle\,,

(66a)

which are proportional to rotation matrix elements in two
different

bases: the original

\mathbf{z}
-axis projection basis for the left index, and the
\mathsf{G}
-adapted basis for the right index.

If the irrep is one-dimensional, then there is no internal irrep index, and the matrix elements are

\langle_{m\kappa}^{J}|\mathsf{r}\rangle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}}}}D_{m;\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{r})\quad\text{(1D irrep)}\quad,

(66b)

with the semicolon and Greek index distinguishing them from the ordinary Wigner
D
-matrices
D^{J}_{mk}
from Eq. (
21
).
The latter are recovered by picking
\mathsf{G}
to be the trivial group (see Example
7
).
For groups such as
\mathsf{C}_{N}
, the adapted matrix elements are a subset of the Wigner elements, in which case we default to the Wigner set.

The above change of basis yields
the following expression for the
\mathsf{G}
-adapted rotational states in
terms of position states,

\displaystyle|_{m\kappa}^{J},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}}}}\int_{\mathsf{SO(3)}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{r}\,D^{J\star}_{m;\nu\kappa}(\mathsf{r})|\mathsf{r}\rangle\,.

(67a)

The inverse expression,

|\mathsf{r}\rangle=\sum_{J\geq|m|\geq 0}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}}}}\sum_{\Gamma\uparrow J}\sum_{\nu=1}^{\dim\Gamma}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\ml_{J}\Gamma}D_{m;\nu\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{r})|_{m\kappa}^{J},\nu\rangle\,,

(67b)

is easily obtained by remembering that the orthogonality and completeness
properties of the Wigner
D
-matrices
Varshalovich et al. 1988
;
Albert et al. 2020
are
maintained under basis changes.

These bases provide an alternative decomposition of the rotation-spin state space of an asymmetric molecule, decomposing the identity from Eq. (
22
) as

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{SO(3)}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{r}\,|\mathsf{r}\rangle\langle\mathsf{r}|\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{nuc}}~,

(68a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\geq|m|\geq 0}\sum_{\Gamma\uparrow J}\sum_{\nu=1}^{\dim\Gamma}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\ml_{J}\Gamma}|_{m\kappa}^{J},\nu\rangle\langle_{m\kappa}^{J},\nu|\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{nuc}}~.

(68b)

IX.2
Rotationally symmetric molecules

The position states of an asymmetric molecule are in one-to-one correspondence
with elements of
\mathsf{SO(3)}
since any proper rotation, by definition,
rotates the molecule from some initial position into a
different

final position.
Rotationally symmetric molecules (see Sec.
VIII
for a definition) admit a proper-rotation
subgroup
\mathsf{G}
that leaves the molecule invariant per the restriction from Eq.
(
30
), so not all
\mathsf{SO(3)}
rotations are needed to label
distinct orientations of a symmetric molecule.

Re-stating the symmetry restriction in terms of group theory, given
a label
\mathsf{a}
for a molecular position state, any labels of the form

\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}
for symmetry-group rotations
\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}
correspond to the
same position and are therefore redundant. This redundancy implies
that each
\mathsf{a}
is representative of its corresponding
left
coset
of
\mathsf{G}
in
\mathsf{SO(3)}
,

\mathsf{a}\mathsf{G}=\{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\,|\,\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}\}\,.

(69)

The set of labels for distinct position states of a
\mathsf{G}
-symmetric
molecule thus corresponds to elements of
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}
, the space of
left cosets of
\mathsf{G}
in
\mathsf{SO(3)}
. Each coset can be represented by
only one of its elements, and particular choices of representatives
are referred to as “sections”, “gauges”, or “transversals”.

Position states
|\mathsf{a}\rangle
of
\mathsf{G}
-symmetric molecules form a subspace
of the state space of an asymmetric molecule
and can be expressed as equal superpositions of elements of their corresponding
cosets,

|\mathsf{a}\rangle\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathsf{G}|}}\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\rangle\,,

(70)

where
|\mathsf{r}\rangle
is a position state of an asymmetric molecule from
Eq. (
67b
), and where
|\mathsf{G}|
is the number of elements in the group.
For continuous groups
\mathsf{G}\in\{\mathsf{C}_{\infty},\mathsf{D}_{\infty}\}
, the sum turns
into an integral, and
|\mathsf{G}|
becomes the group volume; we cover such
cases in the examples.

The above coset states satisfy the symmetry constraint from Eq. (
30
)
since the application of a molecule-based rotation
merely permutes the elements in the superposition.
For any
\mathsf{h}\in\mathsf{G}
, we have

\displaystyle\overleftharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{h}}|\mathsf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathsf{G}|}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\mathsf{g}}}\left|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\mathsf{h}^{-1}\right\rangle

(71a)

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathsf{G}|}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\mathsf{g}}}\left|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\right\rangle~.

(71b)

In the first equality, we use the definition of the action of molecule-based
rotations on asymmetric position states,
\overleftharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}|\mathsf{r}\rangle=|\mathsf{r}\mathsf{g}^{-1}\rangle
in Eq. (
15a
).
In the second, we use the group resummation property,

{\textstyle\sum_{\mathsf{g}}}f(\mathsf{g}\mathsf{h})={\textstyle\sum_{\mathsf{g}}}f(\mathsf{g})\,,

(72)

for any
\mathsf{h}\in\mathsf{G}
and any function
f
on the group.

We now express each position state
|\mathsf{r}\rangle
in the above coset states as a superposition of
\mathsf{G}
-adapted rotational states using Eq. (
67b
).
Rearranging
sums, writing out the
\mathsf{G}
-adapted
D
-matrices (
66
),
and splitting up the product between
\mathsf{a}
and
\mathsf{g}
yields

\displaystyle|\mathsf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\geq|m|\geq 0}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\sum_{\Gamma\uparrow J}\sum_{\nu=1}^{\dim\Gamma}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\ml_{J}\Gamma}

(73)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\Big\langle m\Big|D^{J}(\mathsf{a})\Big[{\textstyle\frac{1}{|\mathsf{G}|}}{\textstyle\sum_{\mathsf{g}}}D^{J}(\mathsf{g})\Big]\Big|\nu\kappa\Big\rangle\,\,|_{m\kappa}^{J},\nu\rangle\,.

This equation can be further simplified by noticing that the sum
in square brackets is a projection onto all copies of the trivial irrep. In
terms of the
\mathsf{G}
-adapted basis,

\frac{1}{|\mathsf{G}|}\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}D^{J}(\mathsf{g})|\nu\kappa\rangle=\delta_{\Gamma,\textsc{a}}|\kappa\rangle\,,

(74)

where the
\nu
index is not present since
\dim\textsc{a}=1
.

Plugging in the expression for the projection reduces the position-state
expression (
73
) in the following
ways. First, the sum over
\Gamma
goes away due to
\Gamma=\textsc{a}
. As a result, the sum over
J
is
reduced to the sum over only those momenta which contain at least
one copy of the trivial irrep; we denote this set by
J\downarrow\textsc{a}
.
Second, the sums over
\nu
and
\kappa
index the irrep and multiplicity
spaces of
a
, and the former goes away since the irrep dimension
is one, i.e.,
|_{m\kappa}^{J},\nu\rangle\to|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle
.
Altogether, this yields

|\mathsf{a}\rangle=\sum_{J\downarrow\textsc{a}}\sum_{|m|\leq J}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\ml_{J}\textsc{a}}D_{m;\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{a})|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle\,,

(75)

which expresses position states precisely in terms of the rotational
states
\{|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle\}
that we obtained in Sec.
VIII.1
.

The symmetric molecular position states (
75
)
constitute an orthonormal “basis” for the state space of a
\mathsf{G}
-symmetric
rigid body,

\langle\mathsf{a}|\mathsf{a}^{\prime}\rangle=\delta^{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}^{\prime})\,,

(76)

where the coset-space Dirac
\delta
-function is infinite for coset representatives

\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{a}^{\prime}
and zero otherwise. Position states resolve the identity
on the symmetric state space as

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}^{\mathsf{G}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}|\mathsf{a}\rangle\langle\mathsf{a}|\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{nuc}}\,,

(77)

dual to the rotational-state identity in Eq. (
36
).

The reverse Fourier transform expressing
|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle
in
terms of position states can be derived by using the fact that cosets
partition
\mathsf{SO(3)}
. Given a fixed set of coset representatives,
each rotation
\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{SO(3)}
can be written as a product of a particular representative
and symmetry-group element,

\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\quad\text{for some}\quad\mathsf{a}\in\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}\,.

(78)

As a result, any integral over
\mathsf{SO(3)}
can be split into an
integral over the coset space and a sum over the symmetry group. Applying
this to Eq. (
67a
) and specializing to the trivial (one-dimensional) irrep yields

|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}}}}\int_{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}D_{m;\kappa}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g})|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\rangle\,~,

(79)

where
\kappa
indexes the multiplicity of
a
.

Next, we simplify the
\mathsf{G}
-adapted matrix elements by noticing that
taking the
\kappa
th element is effectively evaluating
\mathsf{g}
in the
trivial
\mathsf{G}
-irrep,

\displaystyle D_{m;\kappa}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g})

\displaystyle=\langle m|D^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})D^{J\star}(\mathsf{g})|\kappa\rangle

(80a)

\displaystyle=\langle m|D^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})\textsc{a}(\mathsf{g})|\kappa\rangle

(80b)

\displaystyle=\langle m|D^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})|\kappa\rangle\,.

(80c)

Plugging this in, evaluating
\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}1=|\mathsf{G}|
, and expressing the sum of position states
|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\rangle
as a symmetric position state
|\mathsf{a}\rangle
from Eq. (
70
) yields

|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\int_{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\,D_{m;\kappa}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})|\mathsf{a}\rangle\,,

(81)

completing the Fourier transform on
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}
.

Example 25

(calcium monohydrosulfide)
.

The procedure for determining the position state space of a symmetric molecule can also be applied to asymmetric molecules.
Recalling Example
7
, the symmetry group of such molecules is trivial, containing only the identity element,
\mathsf{G}=\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle
.
The coset space
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{C}_{1}}}=\mathsf{SO(3)}
, meaning that the entire group remains as the set of labels of asymmetric molecular position states.

The trivial irrep is the only irrep, so the sum over
\Gamma
in the position-state expression (
67b
) goes away.
The trivial irrep is one-dimensional, so there is also no
\nu
index in that equation.
The
\kappa
index goes from 1 to
2J+1=\ml_{J}\textsc{a}
, reducing Eq. (
67b
) to the position states from Eq. (
20b
).

Example 26

(hydrogen chloride)
.

Position states of any
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
-symmetric molecules from Example
6
are labeled by points on the ordinary two-sphere
\mathsf{S}^{2}
.
In our formalism, the sphere is recovered as the coset space
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{C}_{\infty}}}\cong\mathsf{S}^{2}
and corresponds to a subset of coset representatives.

A convenient partition of
\mathsf{SO(3)}
-rotations into coset representatives
\mathsf{a}
and symmetry-group elements
\mathsf{g}
per Eq. (
78
) can be done using the Euler angle parameterization,
\mathsf{r}=(\alpha\beta\gamma)=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}
, when
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
is the group of rotations around the
\mathbf{z}
-axis.
In that case, the
\gamma
angle is reserved for labeling the subgroup elements
\mathsf{g}
, while the pair
(\alpha\beta)
is retained for the position states
\mathsf{a}
.

For
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
, the position state sum in Eq. (
70
) becomes an integral,

|\mathsf{a}\rangle\equiv{\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathsf{G}|}}}\int_{\mathsf{G}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{G}~|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\rangle\,,

(82)

where the group volume is
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\gamma=2\pi
.
When
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
is the subgroup of
\mathbf{z}
-axis rotations in the Euler-angle parameterization, the above integral reduces to one over
\gamma
.

The
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
-adapted matrix elements correspond to the spherical harmonics,

Y_{m}^{J}(\mathsf{a})={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{4\pi}}}D_{m0}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})~,

(83)

and the position and momentum states for this case are

\displaystyle|\mathsf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\geq 0}\sum_{|m|\leq J}Y_{m}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})|_{m}^{J}\rangle

(84a)

\displaystyle|_{m}^{J}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{S}^{2}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\,Y_{m}^{J}(\mathsf{a})|\mathsf{a}\rangle~,

(84b)

where
|^{J}_{m}\rangle=|^{J}_{m},k=0\rangle
are the rotational basis states determined in Example
6
.

Example 27

(disulfur)
.

The
\text{S}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
molecule, alogn with any spinless homonuclear diatomic, is a
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
-symmetric rigid body (see Example
9
).
Its symmetry group includes the rotations from the previous heteronuclear example, along with all
\pi
-rotations that permute the two identical (spinless) nuclei.

Position states of this case are of the form in Eq. (
82
), with the integral being over
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
, with group volume

|\mathsf{D}_{\infty}|=4\pi
.

The coset space labeling
\text{S}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
positions is
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{D}_{\infty}}}\cong\mathsf{RP}^{2}
— the two-dimensional real projective plane.
This is also known as the two-sphere with opposite points identified,
and each point can be identified with a rod in three-dimensional space
Mermin 1979
.
In terms of the Euler-angle parameterization from the previous example, each projective-plane point
(\alpha\beta)
is a rod with endpoints at
(\alpha\beta)
and its antipode
(\alpha+\pi,\pi-\beta)
.

Position and momentum states for this case are,

\displaystyle|\mathsf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\sum_{J\text{ even}}\sum_{|m|\leq J}Y_{m}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})|_{m}^{J}\rangle

(85a)

\displaystyle|_{m}^{J}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\int_{\mathsf{RP}^{2}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\,Y_{m}^{J}(\mathsf{a})|\mathsf{a}\rangle~,

(85b)

where the square root is due to a different normalization for
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
, i.e.,
8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{D}_{\infty}|=2\pi
.
These are closely related to those of the
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
-symmetric case from Eq. (
84
).
The position states can even be obtained by superposing each
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
-symmetric state
\mathsf{a}=(\alpha\beta)
with its antipode, which restricts the spherical-harmonic sum to only the even-momentum harmonics.

Example 28

(cobalt tetracarbonyl hydride)
.

Recalling Example
10
, this molecule is
\mathsf{C}_{3}
symmetric.
The coset space labeling its positions is
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{C}_{3}}}
, a member of the lens space family,
\mathsf{L}_{2N,1}\equiv\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{C}_{N}}}
.
We handle the general-
N
case directly due to its simple generality.

Picking the group to consist of
\mathbf{z}
-axis rotations, a simple parameterization for the coset space consists of Euler angles
(\alpha\beta\gamma)
, with
0\leq\gamma<2\pi/N
.
The
N\to\infty
case reduces yields the
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
-symmetric Example
26
(cf.
(
Albert et al. 2020
, Appx. D)
).

The symmetry group
\mathsf{C}_{N}
is discrete, so Eq. (
70
) applies directly.
Position and momentum states (with the latter worked out in Example
10
) can be expressed in terms of the standard Wigner
D
-matrix elements,

\displaystyle|\mathsf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\geq 0}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}/N}}}\sum_{|m|\leq J}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{2n+1}D_{m,N(\kappa-n-1)}^{J}(\mathsf{a})|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle

(86a)

\displaystyle|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}/N}}}\int_{\mathsf{L}_{2N,1}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\,D_{m,N(\kappa-n-1)}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})|\mathsf{a}\rangle~,

(86b)

where
n=\lfloor J/N\rfloor
.

Example 29

(sulfur trioxide)
.

Recalling Example
11
, this molecule is
\mathsf{D}_{3}
symmetric.
The coset space labeling its positions is
\mathsf{SO(3)}/\mathsf{D}_{3}
, a member of the prism space family,
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{D}_{N}}}
.
The
N\to\infty
case of such spaces yields the real projective plane from Example
27
.

Recalling the form of the rotational states from Eq. (
39
), the corresponding
\mathsf{D}_{3}
-adapted rotation matrix elements from Eq. (
66b
), expressed in terms of the standard Wigner
D
-matrix elements, are

D_{m;\kappa}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\big(D_{m,3(\kappa-1)}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})-(-1)^{J+\kappa}D_{m,3(1-\kappa)}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})\big),

(87)

for
\mathsf{a}\in\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{D}_{3}}}
.
These are then plugged into the Fourier transform on this prism space,

\displaystyle|\mathsf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\geq 0}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{4\pi^{2}/3}}}\sum_{|m|\leq J}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}D_{m;\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{a})|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle

(88a)

\displaystyle|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{4\pi^{2}/3}}}\int_{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{D}_{3}}}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\,D_{m;\kappa}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})|\mathsf{a}\rangle~.

(88b)

Example 30

(
\text{C}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{60}}}
fullerene)
.

The position-state space of this icosahedrally symmetric molecule is the Poincare homology sphere
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{I}}}

Kirby and Scharlemann 1979
;
Baez 2017
.
This space was once a candidate model for the shape of an assumed-to-be-periodic universe
Luminet et al. 2003
.
Position states are difficult to express in terms of icosahedral harmonics, but admit a simple coset-state expression,

|\mathsf{a}\rangle\equiv{\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{60}}}\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{I}}|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\rangle~.

(89)

IX.3
Perrotationally symmetric molecules

Molecular position states of perrotationally symmetric molecules have
to satisfy the joint symmetry and spin-statistics condition from Eq. (
41
),
under which the collective rotation-spin space transforms according
to the 1D
\mathsf{G}
-irrep
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
. In Sec.
VIII.2
, we showed that there are various
combinations of rotational irreps
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
and nuclear irreps
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
such
that their direct product restricts to
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
.

Here, we develop position states for a single species
s
, which corresponding to the triple
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}\otimes\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}\downarrow\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
.
This requires generalizing
the procedure for rotationally symmetric molecules from the previous
subsection.
The resulting states are still parameterized, in part, by
coset-space labels
\mathsf{a}\in\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}
, but the non-Abelian nature of

\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
irrep attaches extra internal degrees of freedom to each position
state in the entangled case.

IX.3.1
\mathfrak{d}=1
separable species

In the separable case, both irreps
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
and
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
are one-dimensional, and
their tensor product directly yields the correct spin statistics,

\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}\otimes\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
. To identify the position state space, we have
to formulate position states on the rotational factor that transform
according to
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
.

The result is obtained by a simple modification of the coset-state
superposition in Eq. (
70
) to a superposition whose
coefficients are evaluations of the symmetry-group elements in the
desired irrep. For each position-state label
\mathsf{a}\in\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}
, the
state is

|\mathsf{a}\rangle\equiv{\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathsf{G}|}}}\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}(\mathsf{g})\left|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\right\rangle\,.

(90)

These states are Dirac-
\delta
orthogonal, satisfying Eq. (
76
) for any one-dimensional
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
.
When
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\textsc{a}
, they reduce to the rotationally symmetric case in
Eq. (
70
).

The above generalized coset states transform correctly under the rotational part of the perrotation from Eq. (
40
).
Generalizing Eq. (
71
), we have, for any
\mathsf{h}\in\mathsf{G}
,

\displaystyle\overleftharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{h}}|\mathsf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathsf{G}|}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\mathsf{g}}}\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}(\mathsf{g})\left|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\mathsf{h}^{-1}\right\rangle

(91a)

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathsf{G}|}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\mathsf{g}}}\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}(\mathsf{g}\mathsf{h})\left|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\right\rangle

(91b)

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathsf{G}|}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\mathsf{g}}}\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}(\mathsf{g})\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}(\mathsf{h})\left|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\right\rangle

(91c)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}(\mathsf{h})|\mathsf{a}\rangle\,.

(91d)

In the third equality, we recall that
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}

is a scalar representing the group and split the product of
\mathsf{g}

and
\mathsf{h}
.
In the fourth, we re-express the result in terms of the original coset states.

The generalized coset states (
90
) can be expressed
in terms of the rotational states
\{|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle\}
from Eq.
(
47
), where
\kappa
indexes the multiplicity of
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
. The derivation
is a slight generalization of the rotationally symmetric (
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\textsc{a}
)
derivation in Eqs. (
73
-
75
).
We omit it for brevity, noting that the entangled species derivation
done in the next subsection encompasses both.

Position and rotational states for the rotational factor of a separable
species are

\displaystyle|\mathsf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}}\sum_{|m|\leq J}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}D_{m;\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{a})|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle

(92a)

\displaystyle|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\int_{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}D_{m;\kappa}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})|\mathsf{a}\rangle\,.

(92b)

Tacking on the nuclear states
\{|\chi\rangle\}
, which are required
to transform according to
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
, the species’ identity decomposes
as

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}^{\mathsf{G},\textsc{s}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}|\mathsf{a}\rangle\langle\mathsf{a}|\otimes\sum_{\chi=1}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}}|\chi\rangle\langle\chi|\,,

(93)

dual to the rotational-state decomposition from Eq. (
45
).

Example 31

(water,
\textsc{a}^{\ast}
para species)
.

Recalling Example
13
,
\text{H}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}\text{O}
is
\mathsf{C}_{2}
-symmetric, so its position states are parameterized by points in the lens space
\mathsf{L}_{4,1}\equiv\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{C}_{2}}}
.
Using the Euler-angle parameterization from Example
28
,
each coset consists of only two elements,
(\alpha\beta\gamma)
and
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma+\pi)
.
Coset representatives are parameterized by
(\alpha\beta\gamma)
with
\alpha=[0,2\pi)
,
\beta=[0,\pi]
, and
\gamma=[0,\pi)
.

Since
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\textsc{a}
is the trivial irrep for this species, we have
\textsc{a}(\gamma=0)=\textsc{a}(\gamma=\pi)=+1
.
The coset states from (
90
) are

\left|\mathsf{a}=(\alpha\beta\gamma)\right\rangle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\big(\left|\mathsf{r}=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\right\rangle+\left|\mathsf{r}=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma+\pi)\right\rangle\big)~,

(94)

where we abuse notation and use a coset representative’s Euler-angle parameterization as a stand-in for the representative
\mathsf{a}
itself.
Applying the symmetry-group rotation
\overleftharpoon{X}_{00\pi}
to this state yields
+1
, confirming that the state transforms according to the trivial irrep.

The Fourier transform between these states and the rotational states is that from Eq. (
85
) for
N=2
.
Per Example
13
, a basis for the species consists of tensor products of the above states with the singlet state
(\left|\uparrow\downarrow\right\rangle-\left|\downarrow\uparrow\right\rangle)/\sqrt{2}
of the two hydrogen nuclei and an arbitrary state of the oxygen nucleus.

Example 32

(water,
\textsc{b}^{\ast}
ortho species)
.

The other species of water, considered in Example
14
, admit the
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\textsc{b}
irrep, for which
\textsc{b}(\gamma=0)=+1
and
\textsc{b}(\gamma=\pi)=-1
.
Plugging this data into the generalized coset states in Eq. (
90
) yields

\left|\mathsf{a}=(\alpha\beta\gamma)\right\rangle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\big(\left|\mathsf{r}=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\right\rangle-\left|\mathsf{r}=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma+\pi)\right\rangle\big)~.

(95)

Applying
\overleftharpoon{X}_{00\pi}
to this state yields
-1
, confirming that the state transforms according to the
b
irrep.

Per Example
14
, the rotational states transforming according to the
b
irrep are the asymmetric states
|^{J}_{m},k\rangle
for which
k
is odd.
The number of such states for a given
J
is
\mathfrak{m}(J)=2\lfloor(J+1)/2\rfloor
, and the symmetric rotational states are
|^{J}_{m\kappa}\rangle=|^{J}_{m},k=2\kappa-\mathfrak{m}(J)-1\rangle
for
1\leq\kappa\leq\mathfrak{m}(J)
.
The Fourier transform between position and rotational states for this species is then expressible using the Wigner
D
-matrix elements,

\displaystyle|\mathsf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J>0}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{4\pi^{2}}}}\sum_{|m|\leq J}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}D_{m,2\kappa-\mathfrak{m}(J)-1}^{J}(\mathsf{a})|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle

(96a)

\displaystyle|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{4\pi^{2}}}}\int_{\mathsf{L}_{4,1}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\,D_{m,2\kappa-\mathfrak{m}(J)-1}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})|\mathsf{a}\rangle~.

(96b)

Note that all momenta participate except
J=0
, which has no odd values of its
\mathbf{z}
-axis component.

A basis for the species consists of tensor products of the above states with any triplet-subspace state of the two hydrogen nuclei and an arbitrary state of the oxygen.

Example 33

(deuterated hydrogen,
\textsc{a}_{1}
ortho species)
.

Recalling Example
15
,
\text{D}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
is
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
-symmetric, so its position states are parameterized by points in the projective plane
\mathsf{RP}^{2}=\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{D}_{\infty}}}
.
This is the same set of labels as for disulfur in Example
27
.
The rotational-state irrep participating in this species is also trivial,
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\textsc{a}_{1}
, so the Fourier transform on the rotational part of this species is the same as that of
\text{S}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
in Eq. (
85
).

A basis for the species consists of tensor products of the above states with any triplet-subspace state of the two deuterium nuclei.

Example 34

(deuterated hydrogen,
\textsc{a}_{2}
para species)
.

Recalling Example
16
, the other species of
\text{D}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
is constructed by setting both the rotational and nuclear-spin states to transform according to the sign irrep,
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}=\textsc{a}_{2}
.
While the position-state labels,
\mathsf{a}\in\mathsf{RP}^{2}
, remain the same as those of the
\textsc{a}_{1}
species, the rotational-state irrep is different.

Since the irrep is nontrivial, the coset-state superposition is no longer uniform like it was in Eq. (
82
).
Parameterizing
\mathbf{z}
-axis rotations by angle
\gamma
by the Euler-angle triple
(00\gamma)
and equatorial rotations by
(0\pi\gamma)
, position states are parameterized by
\mathsf{a}=(\alpha\beta)
(see Example
27
), and position states from Eq. (
90
) are

\left|(\alpha\beta)\right\rangle\equiv{\textstyle\frac{1}{4\pi}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\textnormal{d}\gamma~\left|\mathsf{r}=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\right\rangle-\left|\mathsf{r}=(\alpha+\pi,\pi-\beta,\gamma)\right\rangle\,,

(97)

where the relative phase is due to the fact that that all equatorial rotations evaluate to
-1
in the irrep.

The Fourier transform between position and momentum states (worked out in Example
16
) is

\displaystyle|\mathsf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\sum_{J\text{ odd}}\sum_{|m|\leq J}Y_{m}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})|_{m}^{J}\rangle

(98a)

\displaystyle|_{m}^{J}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\int_{\mathsf{RP}^{2}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\,Y_{m}^{J}(\mathsf{a})|\mathsf{a}\rangle~.

(98b)

A basis for the species consists of tensor products of the above states with the singlet state of the two deuterium nuclei.

Example 35

(ammonia,
{}^{2}\textsc{e}
species)
.

Recalling Example
15
,
\text{NH}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{3}}}
is
\mathsf{C}_{3}
-symmetric, so its position states are parameterized by points in the lens space
\mathsf{L}_{6,1}\cong\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{C}_{3}}}
.
The symmetry group contains three rotations by
\frac{2\pi}{3}p
for
p\in\{0,1,2\}
, which evaluate to
\exp(-i\frac{2\pi}{3}p)
in the
{}^{2}\textsc{e}
irrep.

Position states from Eq. (
90
) are

\left|\mathsf{a}=(\alpha\beta\gamma)\right\rangle=\sum_{p=0}^{2}e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}p}\left|\mathsf{r}=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma+{\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{3}}p)\right\rangle~,

(99)

parameterized by
\alpha\in[0,2\pi)
,
\beta\in[0,\pi]
, and
\gamma\in[0,2\pi/3)
.
States for the
\mathsf{C}_{N}
-symmetric
{}^{2}\textsc{e}_{1}
species
are obtained by letting
3\to N
and iterating
p
from
0
to
N-1
.

Per Example
17
, the rotational states transforming according to the
{}^{2}\textsc{e}
irrep are the asymmetric states
|^{J}_{m},k\rangle
for which
k\equiv 2
modulo 3.
The admissible rotational states are

|^{J}_{m\kappa}\rangle=|^{J}_{m},k=3\kappa-1-3\lfloor(J+2)/3\rfloor\rangle

(100)

for
|m|\leq J
and
1\leq\kappa\leq\mathfrak{m}(J)=J-\lfloor J/3\rfloor
.
The Fourier transform between position and rotational states for this species is then expressible using the Wigner
D
-matrix elements,

\displaystyle|\mathsf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J>0}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{4\pi^{2}}}}\sum_{|m|\leq J}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}D_{m,3\kappa-1-3\lfloor(J+2)/3\rfloor}^{J}(\mathsf{a})|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle

\displaystyle|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{4\pi^{2}}}}\int_{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{C}_{3}}}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\,D_{m,3\kappa-1-3\lfloor(J+2)/3\rfloor}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})|\mathsf{a}\rangle~.

(101)

IX.3.2
\mathfrak{d}\geq 2
entangled species

The collective rotational and nuclear-spin wavefunction of an entangled
species transforms according the irrep
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
, which is present in
the tensor product of its corresponding
\mathfrak{d}
-dimensional rotational
irrep
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
and a nuclear-spin irrep
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
. We first formulate “uncoupled”
position states on the rotational factor that transform as
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
,
and then perform the projection onto the
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}
irrep according to
the prescription of Sec.
VIII.2
.

Since the rotational irrep is no longer one-dimensional,
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}(\mathsf{g})

is a
\mathfrak{d}
-dimensional matrix with matrix elements

\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\nu}(\mathsf{g})=\langle\mu|\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}(\mathsf{g})|\nu\rangle\,,

(102)

where
\mu,\nu\in\{1,2,\cdots,\mathfrak{d}\}
, and where

\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}
. Defining these elements requires a choice of orthonormal basis
|\mu\rangle
for
the irrep space, but the results below hold for any choice.

Extending the coset-state construction from Eq. (
90
)
to this case yields
\mathfrak{d}^{2}
states for each representative
\mathsf{a}\in\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}
,

|\mathsf{a};\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\nu}\rangle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{|\mathsf{G}|}}}\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\nu}(\mathsf{g})|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\rangle\,,

(103)

with each state corresponding to a particular matrix element.

Molecule-based rotations in the symmetry group leave the position-state
label
\mathsf{a}
invariant, but transform the internal degrees of freedom
at each position. More precisely, rotations act on the
\nu
index
according to
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
.
Generalizing
Eq. (
91
) yields, for any
\mathsf{h}\in\mathsf{G}
,

\displaystyle\overleftharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{h}}|\mathsf{a};\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\nu}\rangle

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{|\mathsf{G}|}}}\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\nu}(\mathsf{g}\mathsf{h})|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\rangle

(104a)

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{|\mathsf{G}|}}}\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}\sum_{\sigma=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\sigma}(\mathsf{g})\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\sigma\nu}(\mathsf{h})|\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\rangle

(104b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\sigma\nu}(\mathsf{h})|\mathsf{a};\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\sigma}\rangle~,

(104c)

where we have resolved the identity on the internal

\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
-irrep space in order to split up the product of
\mathsf{g}
and

\mathsf{h}
.

We now express Eq. (
103
) in terms of rotational states
\{|_{m\kappa}^{J},\mu\rangle\}

from Eq. (
67a
). The derivation proceeds analogously to the rotationally
symmetric case in Eqs. (
73
-
75
)
whilst taking into account the internal
\mu,\nu
-labeled degrees of freedom.

After plugging in the
\mathsf{G}
-adapted expressions for asymmetric position states

|\mathsf{r}\rangle
from Eq. (
67b
), writing
out the
\mathsf{G}
-adapted matrix elements from Eq. (
66
),
and splitting up the product between
\mathsf{a}
and
\mathsf{g}
, Eq. (
103
)
becomes

\displaystyle|\mathsf{a};\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\nu}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\geq|m|\geq 0}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\sum_{\Gamma\uparrow J}\sum_{\sigma=1}^{\dim\Gamma}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\ml_{J}\Gamma}

(105)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\Big\langle m\Big|D^{J}(\mathsf{a})\Big[{\textstyle\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{|\mathsf{G}|}}{\textstyle\sum_{\mathsf{g}}}\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\nu}(\mathsf{g})D^{J}(\mathsf{g})\Big]\Big|\sigma\kappa\Big\rangle\,\,|_{m\kappa}^{J},\sigma\rangle\,.

This equation can be further simplified by noticing that
\mathsf{g}
is evaluated in the
\Gamma
irrep, i.e.,

D^{J}(\mathsf{g})|\sigma\kappa\rangle=\Gamma^{\star}(\mathsf{g})|\sigma\rangle\otimes|\kappa\rangle~,

(106)

per the convention chosen in the isotypic decomposition from Eq. (
31
).
Inserting another resolution of identity on the irrep space and simplifying yields

\displaystyle|\mathsf{a};\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\nu}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\geq|m|\geq 0}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\sum_{\Gamma\uparrow J}~\sum_{\sigma,\tau=1}^{\dim\Gamma}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\ml_{J}\Gamma}

(107)

\displaystyle\,\,D_{m;\tau\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{a})\Big[{\textstyle\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{|\mathsf{G}|}}{\textstyle\sum_{\mathsf{g}}}\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\nu}(\mathsf{g})\Gamma^{\tau\sigma\star}(\mathsf{g})\Big]\,\,|_{m\kappa}^{J},\sigma\rangle\,.

The sum in square brackets is then evaluated using Schur orthogonality, yielding three
\delta
-functions,
\delta_{\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}\Gamma}\delta_{\mu\tau}\delta_{\nu\sigma}
.

The first
\delta
-function kills the sum over
\Gamma
by selecting
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
.
Conversely, the sum over
J
is reduced to a sum over only those momenta which contain at least one copy of
the desired irrep; we denote this set by
J\downarrow\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
.
The latter two
\delta
-functions kill the sums over
\tau
and
\sigma
, respectively, leaving only the multiplicity sum over
\kappa
.
Altogether, Eq. (
105
) is simplified to

|\mathsf{a};\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\nu}\rangle=\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}}\sum_{|m|\leq J}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\,\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}D_{m;\mu\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{a})|_{m\kappa}^{J},\nu\rangle\,.

(108)

expressing position states precisely in terms of the uncoupled rotational
states
\{|_{m\kappa}^{J},\nu\rangle\}
from Eq. (
67a
).

We now couple the above position states to the
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
-irrep
nuclear-spin subspace and restrict the result to the collective
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{mol}}

irrep in order to obtain the correct spin statistics. According to
Sec.
VIII.2
, we couple internal irrep space (indexed above by
\nu
)
to its partner, the
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
-irrep space, and project onto the admissible
rotational states from Eq. (
57
),

|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{\nu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}s_{\nu}|_{m\kappa}^{J},\nu\rangle_{\textnormal{rot}}|\nu\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}\,.

(109)

This coupling takes in the set
\{|\mathsf{a};\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\nu}\rangle\}_{\nu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}

of
\mathfrak{d}
states and yields a single entangled species’ position state,

\displaystyle|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{\nu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}s_{\nu}|\mathsf{a};\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\nu}\rangle_{\textnormal{rot}}|\nu\rangle_{\textnormal{nuc}}

(110a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}}\sum_{|m|\leq J}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}D_{m;\mu\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{a})|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle\,,

(110b)

for each
\mathsf{a}
and
\mu
. While the
\nu
-labeled degree of freedom
has been eliminated due to the enforcement of spin statistics, the

\mu
-labeled degree of freedom remains!

Together with the nuclear basis states indexing copies of
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{nuc}}
, the
identity on an entangled species
s
decomposes in terms of position
states as

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\textnormal{mol}}^{\mathsf{G},\textsc{s}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\sum_{\mu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle\langle\mathsf{a},\mu|\otimes\sum_{\chi=1}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}}|\chi\rangle\langle\chi|\,,

(111)

where the tensor product
is not
between the rotational and nuclear-spin factors since the molecular position states contain a nuclear-spin component.
Each molecular
position
\mathsf{a}\in\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}
carries with it, not only the requisite

\mathfrak{m}_{\textnormal{nuc}}
-dimensional nuclear multiplicity space, but also an internal

\mathfrak{d}
-dimensional pseudo-spin factor, spanned by
\{|\mu\rangle\}_{\mu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}
,
that we call the species’
fiber
.

Position states and their fibers form a orthonormal “basis” for the
species, satisfying

\langle\mathsf{a},\mu|\mathsf{a}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}\rangle=\delta_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\delta^{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}^{\prime})\,.

(112)

Orthogonality and completeness can be proven by observing that the uncoupled position states
\{|\mathsf{a};\Gamma^{\mu\nu}\rangle\}
from Eq. (
103
) — when collected over all irreps, matrix elements, and coset representatives — form an orthonormal “basis” for the state space of an asymmetric molecule that is a special case of the
Zak basis

Zak 1967
;
Jüstel 2018
;
Albert et al. 2020
;
Culf et al. 2022
.
Orthogonality and completeness relations for the Zak basis
(
Albert et al. 2020
, Eq. (125))
imply analogous relations for the coupled states from Eqs. (
111
) and (
112
).

The reverse Fourier transform expressing rotational states
|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle

in terms of position states
|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle
can be derived as follows.
We start with expression (
67a
)
of the uncoupled rotational states for
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}
in terms of an integral superposition over the asymmetric
position states,

|_{m\kappa}^{J},\nu\rangle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}}}}\int_{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}D_{m;\nu\kappa}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g})|\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\rangle\,,

(113)

where we have split the integral into one over the coset space and a sum over the symmetry group according to Eq. (
78
).

The product
\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}
inside the matrix element of
D^{J}
can be split such that Eq. (
106
) can be applied.
This yields

\displaystyle|_{m\kappa}^{J},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{8\pi^{2}}}}\int_{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}\sum_{\mu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}D_{m;\mu\kappa}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\nu}(\mathsf{g})|\mathsf{r}\rangle

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\int_{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\sum_{\mu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}D_{m;\mu\kappa}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})|\mathsf{a};\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}^{\mu\nu}\rangle\,.

(114)

We then plug in the above into Eq. (
109
),
eliminating the
\nu
index, and use Eq. (
110
)
to obtain

|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\int_{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\sum_{\mu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}D_{m;\mu\kappa}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle\,,

(115)

completing the Fourier transform for entangled species.

Absorbing the constants into a
\mathsf{G}
-adapted “harmonic”,

H_{m\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{a},\mu)={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}D_{m;\mu\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{a})\,,

(116)

yields Eq. (
5
) from Sec.
II
.

The
\mathsf{G}
-adapted orthogonality and completeness relations become

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\int_{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\sum_{\mu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}H_{m\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{a},\mu)H_{m^{\prime}\kappa^{\prime}}^{J^{\prime}\star}(\mathsf{a},\mu)}

\displaystyle=\delta_{JJ^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\delta_{\kappa\kappa^{\prime}}

(117a)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}}\sum_{|m|\leq J}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}H_{m\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{a},\mu)H_{m\kappa}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\delta^{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}^{\prime})\,.

(117b)

Example 36

(boron triflouride,
\textsc{e}^{\ast}
species)
.

Recalling Example
20
, this
\mathsf{D}_{3}
-symmetric molecule is the simplest example of a rotation-spin entangled species.
Position states are labeled by points in the prism space
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{D}_{3}}}
.
Because the species is entangled, the molecule’s position states
|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle
have a fiber degree of freedom, with internal index
\mu\in\{1,2\}
.

The species’ Fourier transform is in Eqs. (
110
) and (
115
), with
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}_{3}
.
We will see in the next section that, since the fiber degree of freedom comes from the rotational state space, it will transform in a nontrivial way under lab-based rotations.

X
Holonomy of position states

In Sec.
IX
, we developed admissible position states of rotationally and perrotationally symmetric molecular species and obtained the rotation-spin states
|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle
, tensored with a nuclear-spin factor spanned by appropriately transforming nuclear-spin states
|\chi\rangle
.
Here, we derive the holonomy of such states arising from a closed adiabatic path in position state space, showing that it depends only on the “global” or “topological” details of the path.
Motivated by our results, we also make a conjecture about holonomy in more general situations.

We omit the residual
|\chi\rangle
factor in the position-state expressions from now on because it is decoupled from and not relevant to the calculation.
Since we deal only with the (dressed) rotational factor, we also drop the “rot” subscript, i.e,
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}\to\Gamma
.

X.1
Induced representations

We derive the holonomy only for the case of a general entangled perrotational species.
It position states are

\big\{~|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle\,\,\text{s.t.}\,\,\mathsf{a}\in\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}\text{ and }\mu\in\{1,\cdots,\mathfrak{d}\}~\big\}\,,

(118)

where
\mu
labels the internal degrees of freedom of the rotational

\mathsf{G}
-irrep
\Gamma
, and where
\mathfrak{d}=\dim\Gamma
.
This general case can then be specialized to all other cases of interest.
States of separable species are obtained by setting
\Gamma
to a one-dimensional irrep and removing
the
\mu
index.
Any rotationally symmetric
case is obtained by setting
\Gamma
to be the trivial irrep.

Interpreting the above state set from a geometric perspective, each
point
\mathsf{a}\in\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}
can be thought of as housing the fiber’s
\mathfrak{d}
-dimensional vector space.
Such a space transforms under lab-based rotations
\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}
according to what is known
as the induced representation,
\Gamma\uparrow\mathsf{SO(3)}

Mackey 1952
;
Mackey 1968
;
Coleman 1968
;
Inui et al. 1990
;
Carter et al. 1995
;
Chirikjian and Kyatkin 2000
;
Folland 2016
.

For all trivial-irrep cases considered here, the vector space has
effectively zero dimension, and each state
|\mathsf{a}\rangle
can be thought
of as a point. This corresponds to the representation
\textsc{a}\uparrow\mathsf{SO(3)}
,
encompassing the
\mathsf{G}
-symmetric cases and the
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{C}_{1}
asymmetric
case.

The
\mathfrak{d}=1
case for a nontrivial one-dimensional irrep
\Gamma

yields
\Gamma\uparrow\mathsf{SO(3)}
, corresponding to separable species
of perrotationally symmetric molecules.
In that case, the space is one-dimensional, and lab-based rotations change the global phase of the position states.

The
\mathfrak{d}>1
case corresponds
to the entangled species, and the fiber degrees of freedom transform under lab-based symmetry rotations according to the inducing irrep
(
Albert et al. 2020
, Eq. (131))
.
More generally, assuming sufficiently slow (i.e., adiabatic) movement along
any
path, the resulting transformations from symmetry rotations depend only on the “global” details of the path and not on any small deformations.

X.2
Non-Abelian connection and holonomy

Consider evolving in an adiabatic path in the coset space
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}

of the set of states above parameterized by Eq. (
118
).
We determine the
holonomy for all closed (piece-wise) continuous paths,

\{~\mathsf{a}(t)\,~\text{s.t.}~\,t\in[0,1]~\}\,.

(119)

By closed, we mean that the final point
\mathsf{a}(1)
is identified in
the coset space with the initial point
\mathsf{a}(0)
because of the symmetry of the molecule.
Following Sec.
VII.4
, we parameterize the path using the same coset-state parameterization for all points except, potentially, the last point
\mathsf{a}(1)
.

Upon traversing a closed path, the molecular basis state
|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle

undergoes a holonomy,

|\mathsf{a}(0),\mu\rangle\quad\to\quad U_{\textnormal{hol}}|\mathsf{a}(0),\mu\rangle\,.

(120)

Since there is a vector space for each
\mathsf{a}
, the holonomy is a
\mathfrak{d}
-dimensional
unitary Berry-Wilczek-Zee matrix
Wilczek and Zee 1984
. Generalizing Eq. (
25
),

U_{\textnormal{hol}}=U_{\textnormal{mono}}\mathcal{P}\exp\left(-\int_{\mathsf{a}(0)}^{\mathsf{a}(1)}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{a}\,A(\mathsf{a})\right)\,.

(121)

The second term on the right-hand side is the path-ordered integral
of exponentials of the Berry-Wilczek-Zee connection
A(\mathsf{a})
(we call this the Berry connection from now on). The
connection is now matrix-valued, with elements

A_{\mu\nu}(\mathsf{a})=\frac{i\langle\mathsf{a},\mu|\partial\mathsf{a},\nu\rangle}{\sqrt{\langle\mathsf{a},\mu|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle\langle\mathsf{a},\nu|\mathsf{a},\nu\rangle}}\,,

(122)

all defined for points
\mathsf{a}(t<1)
.
Path-ordering, denoted by
\cal P
, means that matrix exponentials of
A
earlier in the
path
\mathsf{a}(t)
are written before those later in the path.

The monodromy
U_{\textnormal{mono}}
is a compensating matrix that is the result of re-expressing the label
\mathsf{a}(1)
of the final state in terms of the label

\mathsf{a}(0)
of the initial state.
The monodromy is only an issue when the path
is non-contractible, but such paths exist in
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}
for any

\mathsf{G}
that admits perrotationally symmetric molecules (see Tab.
2
).

The set of holonomies for closed paths starting and ending at a particular

base point

\mathsf{a}(0)
forms a group called the
holonomy group

at that point. It is sufficient to know the holonomy group at the identity
base point,
\mathsf{a}(0)=\mathsf{e}
, to obtain the group at all other
basepoints. This is because the space
\mathsf{SO(3)}
admits a path connecting any two points,
so if we know the holonomy group at
\mathsf{e}
, we can obtain
other holonomy groups by shifting via lab-based rotations

\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}
(for some
\mathsf{g}
) to some other desired
base point

(
Gottesman and Zhang 2017
, Eq. (16))
.

We verify that the Berry connection from Eq. (
121
)
is zero for all spaces considered in this work.
In Sec.
X.4
,
we prove this claim for some cases either via a brute-force analytical
calculation or via symmetry arguments, and provide numerical
evidence for the rest of the cases.
Our results are summarized in
Table
6
.

Since the work on the connection is technical, we assume the
connection is zero and proceed directly to calculating the monodromy
in the next subsection. Given such a locally flat connection, the holonomy group
is equal to the
monodromy group
— the group formed by
all monodromies.

Table 6:
Table listing reasons why the Berry connection (
122
) is
proven to be locally flat as well as the monodromy
group
\mathsf{G}_{\textnormal{mono}}
(

) for
the perrotationally symmetric molecular species from Table
