.
The species
\textsc{s}^{\ast}
has the same monodromy group as

s
since the rotational states of both transform under
the same induced representation,
\textsc{s}\uparrow\mathsf{SO(3)}
.
The parameter
M=\text{GCD}(N,i)
for the groups
\mathsf{C}_{M}

and
\mathsf{D}_{M}
.
Flatness of the connection in the cases marked by “
\checkmark
” is proven by symmetry arguments (see
Sec.
X.4.1
), proven by explicit analytical calculation (see Sec.
X.4.2
), or supported by numerical evidence (see Sec.
X.4.4
).
Species with a star in the last column have non-Abelian monodromy groups, while the fibers of those with a filled-in star also form protected encodings (see Sec.
XI
).

X.3
Nontrivial monodromy

Fixing
\mathsf{a}(0)=\mathsf{e}
, we begin with the “identity” coset subspace from Eq. (
110
),

\left|\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{e},\mu\right\rangle=\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}\sum_{|m|\leq J}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}D_{m;\mu\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{e})|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle\,,

(123)

where
\mu\in\{1,2,\cdots,\mathfrak{d}\}
.
We consider paths obtained by applying lab-based rotations in the
symmetry group, i.e.,
\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}
for any
\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}
. Such rotations
parameterize a “direct” path (w.r.t. some metric on the group) from
\mathsf{a}(0)=\mathsf{e}
to
\mathsf{a}(1)=\mathsf{g}
.
Since the underlying molecule is
\mathsf{G}
-symmetric, such paths are in
fact closed in the coset space despite
\mathsf{g}\neq\mathsf{e}
.

For example, a
\pi
-rotation around an axis perpendicular to the primary
axis of
\text{H}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
exchanges the molecule’s nuclei and yields a non-contractible
path in its position state space,
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{D}_{\infty}}}=\mathsf{RP}^{2}
.
Such a path would be considered open if the nuclei were distinguishable,
but it is instead closed in the state space because the nuclei are

in
distinguishable.
This rotation maps the spatial coordinates of one nucleus to those of the other, but acts purely on the rotational factor and
does not
permute any nuclear-spin factors.

When the connection
A=0
, the monodromy for any other
path between
\mathsf{e}
and
\mathsf{g}
is the same as that for the “direct”
path from
\mathsf{e}
to
\mathsf{g}
since the monodromy is independent of smooth
path deformations
Read 2009
;
Gottesman and Zhang 2017
.

Applying
\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}
to the state from Eq. (
123
),
we recall from Eq. (
19a
) that lab-based rotations act on the
|_{m}^{J}\rangle

factor of each rotational state,

\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}|_{m\kappa}^{J}\rangle=\sum_{|n|\leq J}D_{nm}^{J}(\mathsf{g})|_{n\kappa}^{J}\rangle\,.

(124)

Plugging this in and simplifying yields

\displaystyle\!\!\!\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{g}}\left|\mathsf{e},\mu\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}\sum_{n,m}D_{nm}^{J}(\mathsf{g})D_{m;\mu\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{e})|_{n\kappa}^{J}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}\sum_{|n|\leq J}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}D_{n;\mu\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{g})|_{n\kappa}^{J}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left|\mathsf{g},\mu\right\rangle\,.

(125)

Since
\mathsf{g}
is a symmetry rotation, both
\left|\mathsf{e},\mu\right\rangle

and
\left|\mathsf{g},\mu\right\rangle
describe a molecule in the same
physical position. Mathematically, both
\mathsf{g}
and
\mathsf{e}
are in the same (identity) coset,
\mathsf{e}\mathsf{G}=\{\mathsf{h}\in\mathsf{G}\}
.

However, the
\left|\mathsf{g},\mu\right\rangle
state is representing
the coset by
\mathsf{g}
, while the
\left|\mathsf{e},\mu\right\rangle
state
is using the identity
\mathsf{e}
. We have to pick
one
parameterization
to be consistent with our expression of the holonomy, and we pick
the representative of each coset to be the element closest to the
identity (w.r.t. to some metric on the group). Given such
a parameterization, we have to re-express
\left|\mathsf{g},\mu\right\rangle

in terms of
\left|\mathsf{e},\mu\right\rangle
.

Applying Eq. (
106
) and using the fact that
\Gamma^{\nu\mu\star}(\mathsf{g})=\Gamma^{\mu\nu}(\mathsf{g}^{-1})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{g},\mu\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}\sum_{|n|\leq J}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}\sum_{\nu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}D_{n;\nu\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{e})\Gamma^{\nu\mu\star}(\mathsf{g})|_{n\kappa}^{J}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}\Gamma^{\mu\nu}(\mathsf{g}^{-1})\left|\mathsf{e},\nu\right\rangle\,,

(126)

resulting in an operation on the fiber degrees of freedom
\{|\mu\rangle\}
.
This change in the internal state is precisely the monodromy, which
in this case realizes the
\Gamma
irrep of the symmetry group.

The above is true for any
\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}
, so there are at most
|\mathsf{G}|

different types of monodromies. The actual number of distinct monodromies
is equal to the number of distinct irrep elements.
For example, in the case of rotationally symmetric molecules,
all monodromies are
+1
.

Example 37

(rotationally symmetric molecules)
.

In these cases,
\mathsf{G}
is any group, but the induced representation
is induced by the trivial irrep,
\Gamma=\textsc{a}
. The states
\left|\mathsf{a},\mu\right\rangle

reduce to the coset states
|\mathsf{a}\rangle
from Eq. (
70
). The fiber is
one-dimensional (
\mathfrak{d}=1
), so there is no
\mu
index. Since
\Gamma(\mathsf{g})=\textsc{a}(\mathsf{g})=1

for any
\mathsf{g}
, the states
|\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{g}\rangle
and
|\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{e}\rangle
are
directly identified, and there is no monodromy.

The above applies also to the asymmetric case, for which
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{C}_{1}
,
and any perrotationally symmetric species for which
\Gamma
is the trivial
irrep. Hence, the monodromy group in the first line of each symmetry
group in Table
6
is the trivial group,
\mathsf{G}_{\textnormal{mono}}=\mathsf{C}_{1}
.

On the other hand, perrotationally symmetric molecular species exhibit
nontrivial monodromy for all
\Gamma
but the trivial one. Their monodromy
group is the group formed by the distinct elements
\{\Gamma(\mathsf{g})\,,\,\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}\}
.
This is a normal subgroup of
\mathsf{G}
, obtained by taking the quotient
of
\mathsf{G}
by the kernel of
\Gamma
,

\mathsf{G}_{\textnormal{mono}}=\mathsf{G}/\ker\Gamma\,,

(127)

where
\ker\Gamma
is the subset of elements of
\mathsf{G}
which evaluate
to the identity in the
\Gamma
irrep.

Example 38

(water,
\textsc{b}^{\ast}
ortho species)
.

This species, with symmetry group
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{C}_{2}
and irrep
\Gamma=\textsc{b}
, admits
the simplest nontrivial monodromy. The irrep is one-dimensional, so
there is no
\mu
index. The only non-identity symmetry rotation,

\mathsf{g}=(00\pi)
in the Euler-angle prescription,
exchanges the two hydrogen nuclei and evaluates to
-1
in the
b

irrep (see Example
32
).
This yields a monodromy of
-1
, which is not present in the
para species since
\Gamma
is trivial for that species. The monodromy
group is the symmetry group itself,
\mathsf{G}_{\textnormal{mono}}=\mathsf{C}_{2}
.

The
-1
monodromy can alternatively be derived using the coset-state
expression from Eq. (
95
). Beginning with the identity
coset state,
\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{e}=(000)
, and applying the symmetry rotation yields

\displaystyle\overrightharpoon{X}_{(00\pi)}\left|(000)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\overrightharpoon{X}_{(00\pi)}{\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}(\left|\mathsf{r}=(000)\right\rangle-\left|\mathsf{r}=(00\pi)\right\rangle)

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}(\left|\mathsf{r}=(00\pi)\right\rangle-\left|\mathsf{r}=(000)\right\rangle)

\displaystyle=-\left|(000)\right\rangle\,.

(128)

Above, we continue to abuse notation and use a rotation’s Euler-angle parameterization as a stand-in for the rotation
\mathsf{r}
(or
\mathsf{a}
) itself.

Example 39

(deuterated hydrogen,
\textsc{a}_{2}
para species)
.

The coset states of this
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
-symmetric molecule are labeled
by points in the projective plane,
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{D}_{\infty}}}=\mathsf{RP}^{2}

(see Example
34
). The
\textsc{a}_{2}
irrep
is one-dimensional, so there is no
\mu
index in the position states

|\mathsf{a}\rangle
[see Eq. (
98
)].

A
\pi
-rotation around any equatorial axis yields a monodromy
of
-1
, while the
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
subgroup of
\mathbf{z}
-axis rotations
is mapped to
+1
. The monodromy group is
\mathsf{G}_{\textnormal{mono}}=\mathsf{D}_{\infty}/\mathsf{C}_{\infty}=\mathsf{C}_{2}
.
This is the same monodromy group as that for the
\textsc{a}_{2}^{\star}

(ortho) species of ordinary
\text{H}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
since the rotational state
subspace —
\textsc{a}_{2}\uparrow\mathsf{SO(3)}
— is the same
for both species.

We easily extract the monodromy using momentum states. The identity position state from Eq. (
98
) is

\left|\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{e}\right\rangle=\sum_{J\,\text{odd}}{\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{2J+1}{2\pi}}}~|_{0}^{J}\rangle~.

(129)

Applying a
\pi
-rotation around the
\mathbf{y}
axis and using the fact that
\overrightharpoon{X}_{(0\pi 0)}|_{0}^{J}\rangle=\left(-1\right)^{J}|_{0}^{J}\rangle
yields the
-1
phase.
A
\pi
-rotation around any equatorial axis yields the same phase since such a rotation can be expressed as a product of
\overrightharpoon{X}_{(0\pi 0)}
and a rotation around the
\mathbf{z}
axis.

In contrast, position states of ortho deuterium and para hydrogen are of the same form as above, except that only even momenta are present.
The monodromy for those cases is trivial.

Example 40

(ammonia,
{}^{2}\textsc{e}
species)
.

This species, with symmetry group
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{C}_{3}
and irrep
\Gamma={}^{2}\textsc{e}
,
is the simplest to realize a monodromy that is not
\pm 1
, i.e.,
that is not related to Bose/Fermi spin statistics.

The
{}^{2}\textsc{e}
irrep is one-dimensional, so there is no
\mu
index
in the position states
|\mathsf{a}\rangle
[see Eq. (
98
)].
The irrep evaluates to third roots of unity and has a trivial kernel,
yielding the monodromy group
\mathsf{G}_{\textnormal{mono}}=\mathsf{C}_{3}
. The
\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{e}=(000)

position state transforms under a symmetry rotation as

\displaystyle\overrightharpoon{X}_{00\frac{2\pi}{3}}\left|(000)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{2}e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}p}\left|\mathsf{r}=(0,0,{\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{3}}p-{\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{3}})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}}\left|(000)\right\rangle\,.

(130)

Example 41

(boron trifluoride,
e
species)
.

Per Example
20
, this perrotationally
\mathsf{D}_{3}
-symmetric species admits a
\mathfrak{d}=2
-dimensional fiber — the simplest fiber with non-unity dimension.
Rotations permuting its three fluorine nuclei realize the group’s two-dimensional irrep
e
from Eq. (
49
) via monodromy.

Example 42

(methane,
t
species)
.

Per Example
21
, the position state space of methane is parameterized by the octahedral space
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{T}}}
, with fiber degrees of freedom
|\mu\rangle
with
\mu\in\{1,2,3\}
.
Rotations permuting its four hydrogen nuclei realize the group’s three-dimensional irrep
t
via monodromy.

Example 43

(
\text{${}^{13}$C}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{60}}}
fullerene,
h
species)
.

Per Example
24
, the position state space of isotopic fullerene is parameterized by the Poincare dodecahedral space
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{I}}}
, with fiber degrees of freedom
|\mu\rangle
with
\mu\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
.
Rotations permuting its sixty hydrogen nuclei realize the group’s five-dimensional irrep
h
via monodromy.
This irrep has a trivial kernel, so the monodromy group is
\mathsf{I}
.

The remaining species, with exception of the trivial-irrep species, also realize the entire icosahedral group as their monodromy group.
This is because this group has no normal subgroups other than itself and the trivial group, which implies that every non-trivial irrep must have trivial kernel.

X.4
Locally flat connection

We proceed to simplify the expression for the connection in Eq. (
122
).
The denominator is a function of state normalizations, which can be
simplified to the formal sum

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{a},\mu|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}\sum_{|m|\leq J}{\textstyle\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}D_{m;\mu\kappa}^{J\star}(\mathsf{a})D_{m;\mu\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{a})

(131a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}{\textstyle\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}\langle\mu\kappa|D^{J}(\mathsf{a}^{-1}\mathsf{a})|\mu\kappa\rangle

(131b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}{\textstyle\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}\mathfrak{m}(J)\,.

(131c)

This sum is formal because it does not converge — the position
states are not normalizable — but this will not be relevant until
we perform numerical calculations in Sec. (
X.4.4
).
For now, we merely keep in mind that the sum is independent of fiber
index
\mu
.

The numerator is simplified to the following form,

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{a},\mu|\partial\mathsf{a},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}{\textstyle\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}\langle\mu\kappa|D^{J\dagger}(\mathsf{a})\partial D^{J}(\mathsf{a})|\nu\kappa\rangle

(132a)

\displaystyle=-i\sum_{\mathbf{q}}\partial w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathsf{a})\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}{\textstyle\frac{(2J+1)/\mathfrak{d}}{8\pi^{2}/|\mathsf{G}|}}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}\langle\mu\kappa|L_{\mathbf{q}}^{J}|\nu\kappa\rangle\,,

(132b)

where
we expand the angular velocity matrix

D^{\dagger}\partial D
in terms of
\mathsf{SO(3)}
angular momentum generators
L_{\mathbf{q}}^{J}

with
\mathbf{q}\in\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}\}
and coordinate vector
\partial w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathsf{a})
.

Plugging the numerator (
132
) and denominator (
131
)
into the connection Eq. (
122
) yields the
expansion

A_{\mu\nu}(\mathsf{a})={\textstyle\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{|\mathsf{G}|}}\sum_{\mathbf{q}}\partial w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathsf{a})\frac{\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}\left(2J+1\right)A_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbf{q},J}}{\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}\left(2J+1\right)\mathfrak{m}(J)}\,,

(133)

with two expressions for the
J
-dependent component,

\displaystyle A_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbf{q},J}

\displaystyle=\frac{|\mathsf{G}|}{\mathfrak{d}}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}\langle\mu\kappa|L_{\mathbf{q}}^{J}|\nu\kappa\rangle

(134a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}\Gamma^{\nu\mu}(\mathsf{g})\text{tr}\left(D^{J}(\mathsf{g})L_{\mathbf{q}}^{J}\right)\,.

(134b)

The second expression is obtained by expressing the sum over
\kappa

as a projection onto the
\nu,\mu
th matrix element of all copies
of
\Gamma
present for a given angular momentum
(
Arovas
, Eq. (2.63))
(
Harter 1993
, Eq. (5.5.20))
.

For each
J
, the component
A_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbf{q},J}
can be thought
of as a three-dimensional vector (with components indexed by
\mathbf{q}
)
of
\mathfrak{d}
-dimensional matrices (with elements indexed by
\mu,\nu
).
Since
\sum_{\mathbf{q}}\partial w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathsf{a})A^{\mathbf{q},J}
generates a unitary
holonomy,
A^{\mathbf{q},J}
must be an element of the Lie
algebra of the unitary group
\mathsf{U}({\mathfrak{d}})
.

In Sec.
X.4.1
, we show that symmetries
constrain
A^{\mathbf{q},J}
to lie in particular Lie subalgebras and,
in some cases, to be identically zero. In Sec.
X.4.2
,
we calculate
A_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbf{q},J}
by hand for certain cases. In
Sec.
X.4.4
, we numerically evaluate the
entire sum ratio
A_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbf{q}}
for the remaining cases.

X.4.1
Symmetries of the connection

Certain combinations of groups and irreps yield a connection of zero for each momentum
J
, which can be proven using various symmetry arguments.

Suppressing the
J
index for the component from Eq. (
134b
),
let us define the following trace quantity,

A^{\mathbf{q}}=\sum_{\mu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}A_{\mu\mu}^{\mathbf{q},J}=\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}\chi_{\Gamma}(\mathsf{g})\text{tr}\left(D^{J}(\mathsf{g})L_{\mathbf{q}}^{J}\right)\,,

(135)

where
\chi_{\Gamma}(\mathsf{g})
is the character of
\mathsf{g}
in the
\Gamma
irrep.
This quantity is invariant under
\mathsf{g}\to\mathsf{h}\mathsf{g}\mathsf{h}^{-1}
for any
\mathsf{h}\in\mathsf{G}
.

To show this, we use the fact that characters are invariant under
conjugation,
\chi(\mathsf{h}\mathsf{g}\mathsf{h}^{-1})=\chi(\mathsf{g})
, and that the vector

(L_{\mathbf{x}}^{J},L_{\mathbf{y}}^{J},L_{\mathbf{z}}^{J})
for any
J
transforms
under the defining rotation-matrix representation
R
of
\mathsf{SO(3)}

(also called the
adjoint representation

Georgi 2021
),

D^{J}(\mathsf{h}^{-1})L_{\mathbf{q}}^{J}D^{J}(\mathsf{h})=\sum_{\mathbf{r}}R_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{r}}(\mathsf{h})L_{\mathbf{r}}^{J}\,.

(136)

Above,
R_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{r}}(\mathsf{h})
are real rotation-matrix elements of the
rotation corresponding to
\mathsf{h}
.

Letting
\mathsf{g}\to\mathsf{h}\mathsf{g}\mathsf{h}^{-1}
and using the above facts,

A^{\mathbf{q}}=\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}\chi_{\Gamma}(\mathsf{h}\mathsf{g}\mathsf{h}^{-1})\text{tr}\left(D^{J}(\mathsf{h}\mathsf{g}\mathsf{h}^{-1})L_{\mathbf{q}}^{J}\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{r}}R_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{r}}(\mathsf{h})A^{\mathbf{r}}.

(137)

This formula is independent of both
\Gamma
and
J
, so any consequences
of it must hold for all irreps and momenta.

Explicitly expressing the connection in vector form,

|A\rangle=A^{\mathbf{x}}|\mathbf{x}\rangle+A^{\mathbf{y}}|\mathbf{y}\rangle+A^{\mathbf{z}}|\mathbf{z}\rangle=\sum_{\mathbf{q}}A^{\mathbf{q}}|\mathbf{q}\rangle\,.

(138)

Such a vector is invariant under the adjoint representation of
\mathsf{G}\subset\mathsf{SO(3)}
,

\displaystyle R(\mathsf{h})|A\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r}}A^{\mathbf{q}}R_{\mathbf{r}\mathbf{q}}(\mathsf{h})|\mathbf{r}\rangle

(139a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{r}}A^{\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{r}\rangle=|A\rangle\,,

(139b)

implying that
|A\rangle
transforms according to the trivial irrep

a
of
\mathsf{G}
.

The main consequence of the above invariance occurs for groups for
which the adjoint representation of
\mathsf{SO(3)}
(which is isomorphic
to the
J=1
irrep) does
not
branch to trivial
\mathsf{G}
-irrep,

J=1\,\,\,\cancel{\downarrow}\,\,\,\textsc{a}\,.

(140)

In other words, if the trivial
\mathsf{G}
-irrep is not present in the adjoint
representation,
|A\rangle
must be the zero vector, i.e.,
A^{\mathbf{q}}=0
.
Equation (
140
) holds for groups
\mathsf{G}\in\{\mathsf{D}_{N},\mathsf{D}_{\infty},\mathsf{T},\mathsf{O},\mathsf{I}\}
.

For 1D irreps
\Gamma
,
A^{\mathbf{q}}=0
means that the entire connection
is zero. This concludes our proof of local flatness for all species
whose rotational states transform according to representations induced
by
\Gamma
.
We have marked such cases by “symmetry” in Table
6
.

For irreps of higher dimension, the tracelessness of
A^{\mathbf{q}}
means
that the holonomy does not contain a global
\mathsf{U}({1})
component, restricting

A_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbf{q},J}
to be an element of
\mathsf{SU}(\mathfrak{d})
(as opposed
to
\mathsf{U}(\mathfrak{d})
).

Another useful piece of information is obtained for groups
\mathsf{O}
and

\mathsf{I}
by considering higher-order invariances. We construct nine-dimensional
and 27-dimensional vectors, respectively,

\displaystyle|A_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r}}\text{tr}_{\Gamma}\left(A^{\mathbf{q}}.A^{\mathbf{r}}\right)|\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r}\rangle

(141a)

\displaystyle|A_{3}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}}\text{tr}_{\Gamma}\left(A^{\mathbf{q}}.A^{\mathbf{r}}.A^{\mathbf{s}}\right)|\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\rangle\,,

(141b)

where the product denoted by “
.
” is over the irrep, i.e.,

\displaystyle\text{tr}_{\Gamma}\left(A^{\mathbf{q}}.A^{\mathbf{r}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu,\nu=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}A_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbf{q},J}A_{\nu\mu}^{\mathbf{r},J}

(142a)

\displaystyle\text{tr}_{\Gamma}\left(A^{\mathbf{q}}.A^{\mathbf{r}}.A^{\mathbf{s}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu,\nu,\sigma=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}A_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbf{q},J}A_{\nu\sigma}^{\mathbf{r},J}A_{\sigma\mu}^{\mathbf{s},J}\,.

(142b)

These vectors are invariant under tensor products of the adjoint representation,

R^{\otimes j}(\mathsf{h})|A_{j}\rangle=|A_{j}\rangle\quad\quad\text{for}\quad\quad j\in\{2,3\}\,.

(143)

In other words,
|A_{j}\rangle
transforms under the trivial irrep of
the
j
th tensor-product representation
R^{\otimes j}
of
\mathsf{G}
. This
invariance can be naturally extended to higher
j
.

The groups
\mathsf{O}
and
\mathsf{I}
contain only one copy of their trivial
irrep in each case. In other words, the second and third tensor product
of the adjoint irrep branches to only one copy of the trivial
\mathsf{O}

and
\mathsf{I}
irrep, respectively,

\displaystyle 1\otimes 1

\displaystyle\downarrow\textsc{a}

(144a)

\displaystyle 1\otimes 1\otimes 1

\displaystyle\downarrow\textsc{a}\,.

(144b)

Since we know the exact form of the rotation matrices
R
, we can
determine the corresponding form of the irrep vector
Ceulemans 2013
. The form of this vector constrains the tracial components, respectively,

\displaystyle\text{tr}_{\Gamma}\left(A^{\mathbf{q}}.A^{\mathbf{r}}\right)

\displaystyle\propto c\delta_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{r}}

(145a)

\displaystyle\text{tr}_{\Gamma}\left(A^{\mathbf{q}}.A^{\mathbf{r}}.A^{\mathbf{s}}\right)

\displaystyle\propto c^{\prime}\epsilon_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{r}\mathbf{s}}\,,

(145b)

where
c,c^{\prime}
are
\mathbf{q}
-independent constants, and
\epsilon

is the fully anti-symmetric tensor. These two constraints restrict

A_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbf{q},J}
to be an element of
\mathsf{SU}(2)

Georgi 2021

(as opposed to
\mathsf{SU}(\mathfrak{d})
) for the groups
\mathsf{O}
and
\mathsf{I}
.

For the remaining exceptional subgroup —
\mathsf{T}
— we contend
with looser constraints
Ceulemans 2013
,

\displaystyle 1\otimes 1

\displaystyle\downarrow 2\textsc{a}

(146a)

\displaystyle 1\otimes 1\otimes 1

\displaystyle\downarrow\textsc{a}\,,

(146b)

which are not sufficient to restrict
A_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbf{q},J}

to be inside
\mathsf{SU}(2)
(although this is something that we observe
numerically).

There are two more special cases that have zero connection component for each
\mathbf{q}
and
J
due to
other types of symmetry.

First, a special symmetry of occurs for the two-dimensional
e
irrep of the octahedral group (cf.
Gross 2021
).
We embed the group into
\mathsf{SO(3)}
such that this irrep maps to identity the rotations
(\mathbf{x},\pi)
,
(\mathbf{y},\pi)
, and
(\mathbf{z},\pi)
.
Each of these rotations, which generate the
\mathsf{D}_{2}
group, switches the sign of one of the three angular momentum generators,
L_{\mathbf{q}}^{J}\to-L_{\mathbf{q}}^{J}
for
\mathbf{q}\in\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}\}
.
This means that we are free to flip the sign of each connection component,
A_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbf{q},J}=-A_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbf{q},J}
, implying that the entire connection is zero for this case.

Second, the
\textsc{t}_{2}
irrep of
\mathsf{I}
admits a property — automatic protection
Kubischta and Teixeira 2023
;
Kubischta and Teixeira 2024
— which guarantees a zero connection component.
Automatic protection occurs only for certain irreps of certain groups, e.g., a particular irrep of the double icosahedral group,
2\mathsf{I}

(
Kubischta and Teixeira 2023
, Thm. 4)
Kubischta and Teixeira 2024
.
We verify that the same property holds for the
\textsc{t}_{2}
irrep of
\mathsf{I}
.

X.4.2
Analytical results

The connection
A_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbf{q},J}
can be evaluated analytically
in certain simple cases, such as when
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{C}_{N}
. We pick this group
to consist of rotations by multiples of
2\pi/N
around the
\mathbf{z}
-axis,
but the result is basis-independent.

All
\mathsf{C}_{N}
-irreps
\{\textsc{a},\textsc{b},~^{j}\textsc{e}_{i}\}
are one-dimensional, so
there are no
\mu,\nu
indices. The expression (
134a
)
for the connection component simplifies to

A^{\mathbf{q},J}=N\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}\langle\kappa|L_{\mathbf{q}}^{J}|\kappa\rangle\,.

(147)

Recalling Eq. (
32
),
\mathsf{C}_{N}
-irrep states
|\kappa\rangle

correspond directly to asymmetric states
|k\rangle
for some
k
.
The sum reduces to

A^{\mathbf{q},J}=N\sum_{|k|\leq J}\delta_{ki}^{\text{mod}N}\langle k|L_{\mathbf{q}}^{J}|k\rangle\,,

(148)

where
\delta_{ki}^{\text{mod}N}=1
when
k\equiv i
modulo
N
,
and zero otherwise.

The value of
i
depends on the irrep, coming from the irrep label
in the case of
\Gamma={}^{1}\textsc{e}_{i}
. The value flips sign when
\Gamma={}^{2}\textsc{e}_{i}
,
it is zero for the trivial irrep
a
, and it is
N/2
for the
b

irrep (for which
N
is even).

Recalling expressions of the angular momentum matrices in the
|k\rangle

basis
Varshalovich et al. 1988
,
L_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}
have nonzero entries only on the bands
above and below the diagonal, meaning that
A^{\mathbf{x},J}=A^{\mathbf{y},J}=0
.
Plugging in
L_{\mathbf{z}}=\sum_{k}|k\rangle k\langle k|
, we see that the
connection is an element of
\mathsf{U}_{1}
,

A^{\mathbf{q},J}=\delta_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{z}}N\sum_{|k|\leq J}\delta_{ki}^{\text{mod}N}k\,.

(149)

The trivial irrep occurs whenever
k\equiv 0
modulo
N
, while
the
b
irrep occurs for
k\equiv N/2
. Both cases have the property
that
k\equiv i
if and only if
-k\equiv i
modulo
N
for
i\in\{N,N/2\}
,
respectively. Therefore, the positive and negative
k
values cancel, yielding

A^{\mathbf{q},J}=0
for these irreps. This concludes our proof for flatness
of connection for all
a
and
b

\mathsf{C}_{N}
-species; they are marked
by “analytics” in the appropriate rows of Table
6
.

The above quantity is not zero for the other irreps, so we have to consider the sum over all
J
.
Plugging in the sum for the connection components from Eq. (
149
)
into the formula for the connection in Eq. (
133
)
yields

A^{\mathbf{q}}=\delta_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{z}}\frac{\sum_{J\geq i}\left(2J+1\right)\sum_{|k|\leq J}\delta_{ki}^{\text{mod}N}k}{\sum_{J\geq i}\left(2J+1\right)\sum_{|k|\leq J}\delta_{ki}^{\text{mod}N}}\,,

(150)

valid for the
{}^{1}\textsc{e}_{i}
irreps of
\mathsf{C}_{N}
(with
i\to-i
for
the
{}^{2}\textsc{e}_{i}
irreps).
We find that this sum is also zero
Kubischta 2023
.

The same sum occurs for the two-dimensional
\textsc{e}_{i}
irreps of
\mathsf{D}_{N}
,
which we take to consist of rotations around the
\mathbf{z}
axis as before.
Each copy is spanned by two states, and we have to be consistent with
the order of the internal indices
\mu,\nu
across all copies. Irrep
copies are spanned by states
\left|k=\pm s\right\rangle
for
any positive
s
such that
s\equiv\min(i,N-i)
modulo
N
, but
are spanned by
\left|k=\mp s\right\rangle
for any positive
s

such that
s\equiv\max(i,N-i)
modulo
N
. Given that off-diagonal
elements
A_{12}^{\mathbf{z}}=A_{21}^{\mathbf{z}}=0
by the same arguments as for
the
\mathsf{C}_{N}
case, calculating the diagonal elements
A_{\mu\mu}^{\mathbf{z}}

yields the sum from Eq. (
150
), up to an overall constant.
We mark the appropriate
\mathsf{C}_{N}
and
\mathsf{D}_{N}
cases with “analytics” in Table
6
.

X.4.3
Approximate states

All Berry connections, including both the ones we have calculated so far and the remaining ones marked by “numerics” in Table
6
, can be calculated numerically using approximate normalizable versions of molecular position states.
The approximate states defined below are straightforward generalizations of finite-energy GKP states
Gottesman et al. 2001
;
Menicucci 2014
and their molecular-code extensions
Albert et al. 2020
;
Culf et al. 2022
.

Regularization proceeds by applying a damping operator to the original position states,

|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle~\to~e^{-\Delta\hat{\mathbf{J}}^{2}/2}|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle\,,

(151)

where the parameter
\Delta\geq 0
, and where

\hat{\mathbf{J}}^{2}=\sum_{J,m,k}J(J+1)|_{m}^{J},k\rangle\langle_{m}^{J},k|

(152)

is the total angular momentum operator squared.
This type of regularization converts delta-function-like states
|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle
into wavepackets in position space centered around the point
\mathsf{a}
.
These states are no longer exactly orthogonal in the coset space, but their overlap is suppressed exponentially with
1/\Delta^{2}
(cf.
Albert et al. 2020
).

Approximate states remain infinite superpositions of momentum states, but are “regularized” in a way that makes them have finite normalization, finite mean angular momentum, and finite values for all higher-order moments in momentum.
The damping operator commutes with all rotations because it is a function of the total angular momentum, so moments of the momentum reduce to ratios of sums that can be bounded.
For example, the average momentum squared reduces to

\langle\hat{\mathbf{J}}^{2}\rangle=\frac{\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}e^{-\Delta J\left(J+1\right)}{\textstyle(2J+1)}\mathfrak{m}(J)J\left(J+1\right)}{\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}e^{-\Delta J\left(J+1\right)}{\textstyle(2J+1)}\mathfrak{m}(J)}~.

(153)

Each sum is finite since each multiplicity
\mathfrak{m}\leq 2J+1
.

X.4.4
Numerical evidence

Connections for the remaining multi-dimensional irreps of
\mathsf{G}\in\{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{O},\mathsf{I}\}

are neither zero for each momentum nor simple to handle analytically, so we calculate them numerically.
The symmetry arguments in Sec.
X.4.1

dictate that we only need to determine some minimal set of matrix elements
\{\mu,\nu\}
since all other elements are determined from them due to the connection belonging to a particular Lie algebra.

Substituting the approximate states from Eq. (
151
) yields a modified, convergent ratio of sums in the connection formula (
133
),

\frac{\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}e^{-\Delta J(J+1)}\left(2J+1\right)A_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbf{q},J}}{\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}e^{-\Delta J(J+1)}\left(2J+1\right)\mathfrak{m}(J)}\,,

(154)

which we calculate numerically to go to zero in the
\Delta\to 0
limit.
We mark these cases with “numerics” in Table
6
.

We observe that all calculated connections converge to zero in a way that is exponential in a power of
1/\Delta
(cf.
Albert et al. 2020
;
Culf et al. 2022
), demonstrating a robustness of our holonomy to finite-energy effects.

X.5
Flat connection conjecture

Induced representation state spaces,
\Gamma\uparrow\mathsf{K}
for Lie group
\mathsf{K}
and
\mathsf{G}
-irrep
\Gamma
, are examples of
\mathsf{G}
-bundles — fiber bundles whose base space is
\mathsf{K}/\mathsf{G}
and whose fiber houses the irrep.
Such bundles come with their own canonical or
\mathsf{G}
-connection,
A^{\mathsf{G}}
.
The Berry connection
A
can be expressed as a sum of two terms, one of which is the
\mathsf{G}
-connection
(
Levay et al. 1996
, Eq. (4.2))
,

A=A^{\mathsf{G}}+A^{\perp}~.

(155)

If we are dealing with Lie groups, then
A^{\mathsf{G}}
is a projection of
\mathsf{K}
’s Maurer-Cartan form into
\mathsf{G}
’s Lie algebra, while
A^{\perp}
is the same projection into the remaining Lie algebra generators of
\mathsf{K}
.

The Berry and
\mathsf{G}
-connections are generally not equal due to the difference term
A^{\perp}
.
We observe that a locally flat Berry connection seems to be synonymous with a locally flat
\mathsf{G}
-connection.
In other words, all three terms in Eq. (
155
) wind up being zero whenever either
A
or
A^{\mathsf{G}}
is zero.

Locally flat
\mathsf{G}
-connections on
\mathsf{G}
-bundles are in one-to-one correspondence with irreps
\rho
of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\mathsf{K}/\mathsf{G})
of the underlying coset space
(
Milnor 1958
, Lemma 1)
.
These irreps generate different types of monodromies occurring after closed non-contractible paths
(
Sudarshan et al. 1988
, Eq. (1))
.
They induce faithful irreps of their corresponding monodromy groups,
\pi_{1}(\mathsf{K}/\mathsf{G})/\ker\rho
.

Per Eq. (
X.3
), our (Berry) monodromies are
also
generated by irreps
\Gamma
, but of a
different
group, namely,
\mathsf{G}
.
These irreps induce faithful irreps of their corresponding monodromy groups,
\mathsf{G}/\ker\Gamma=\mathsf{G}_{\textnormal{mono}}
.

When the two monodromy groups are equal, the two irreps are related to each other,

{\mathsf{G}}/{\ker\Gamma}\cong{\pi_{1}(\mathsf{K}/\mathsf{G})}/{\ker\rho}
.
In other words, given a
\mathsf{G}
-irrep
\Gamma
, there exists a
\pi_{1}
-irrep
\rho
such that above holds, and visa versa.
The
\mathsf{G}
-connection is flat in this case
(
Milnor 1958
, Lemma 1)
, but the Berry connection can nevertheless be nonzero due to the additional term
A^{\perp}
.

We observe that, not only
A^{\mathsf{G}}
, but
all
terms in Eq. (
155
) are zero when the two monodromy groups are isomorphic, leading us to make the following conjecture.

Conjecture
.

Given the state space
\Gamma\uparrow\mathsf{K}
, the position-state Berry connection is locally flat iff there exists a
\pi_{1}(\mathsf{K}/\mathsf{G})
-irrep
\rho
such that

\frac{\mathsf{G}}{\ker\Gamma}\cong\frac{\pi_{1}(\mathsf{K}/\mathsf{G})}{\ker\rho}~.

(156)

We corroborate this conjecture with some examples; see Refs.
Vinet 1988
;
Giler et al. 1989
;
Levay 1994
;
Levay et al. 1996
;
Levay 1995
;
Giller et al. 1993
for more examples.

Example 44

(
\mathsf{K}=\mathsf{SO(3)}
,
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
)
.

The coset space is the two-sphere,
\mathsf{K}/\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{S}^{2}
, whose fundamental group is trivial,
\pi_{1}(\mathsf{S}^{2})=\mathsf{C}_{1}
. Therefore,
\rho=\textsc{a}
, the trivial irrep.

When the
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
-irrep
\Gamma
is trivial, the monodromy group is also trivial per Eq. (
X.3
), and Eq. (
156
) becomes

\frac{\mathsf{C}_{\infty}}{\mathsf{C}_{\infty}}\cong\mathsf{C}_{1}\cong\frac{\mathsf{C}_{1}}{\mathsf{C}_{1}}~.

(157)

This corresponds to a locally flat Berry connection per Table
6
.

All other
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
-irreps
\Gamma=\lambda
(with
\lambda
a non-zero integer) have a kernel of
\mathsf{C}_{|\lambda|}
.
In such cases, repeating the analysis behind Eq. (
X.3
) yields a monodromy group of
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}/\mathsf{C}_{|\lambda|}\cong\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
, and the right-hand side of Eq. (
156
) cannot be satisfied.
Per our conjecture, this means the Berry connection is nonzero.
Indeed, these well-known cases describe a sphere with a “mononople” of
\lambda
, whose Berry phase depends on the area enclosed by the path
Berry 1984
.
In other contexts,
\lambda\uparrow\mathsf{SO(3)}
is called the spin-weighted representation
(
Marinucci and Peccati 2011
, Sec. 12.3)
Fan et al. 2023
.

Example 45

(
\mathsf{K}=\mathsf{SO(3)}
,
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
)
.

The coset space is the projective plane,
\mathsf{K}/\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{RP}^{2}
, whose fundamental group is
\pi_{1}(\mathsf{RP}^{2})=\mathsf{C}_{2}
. Therefore,
\rho
can be either the trivial irrep
a
or the sign irrep
b
.

When the
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
-irrep
\Gamma
is trivial, the monodromy group is also trivial, and Eq. (
156
) becomes

\frac{\mathsf{D}_{\infty}}{\mathsf{D}_{\infty}}\cong\mathsf{C}_{1}\cong\frac{\mathsf{C}_{2}}{\ker\rho}~.

(158)

This is satisfied by picking
\rho
to be trivial, and corresponds to a locally flat Berry connection per Table
6
.

When
\Gamma=\textsc{a}_{2}
(the sign irrep), the monodromy group is non-trivial. Equation (
156
) becomes

\frac{\mathsf{D}_{\infty}}{\mathsf{C}_{\infty}}\cong\mathsf{C}_{2}\cong\frac{\mathsf{C}_{2}}{\ker\rho}~,

(159)

which is satisfied for
\rho=\textsc{b}
. This corresponds to locally flat Berry connection per Table
6
.

All other two-dimensional irreps of
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
yield a monodromy group that is neither
\mathsf{C}_{1}
nor
\mathsf{C}_{2}
.
We cannot satisfy Eq. (
156
) because we have run out of fundamental-group irreps
\rho
.
Per our conjecture, these must a nonzero Berry connection.
This can be verified by repeating the calculations of the previous subsection for this case.

Example 46

(
\mathsf{K}=\mathsf{SO(3)}
,
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{I}
)
.

The coset space is
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{I}}}
, whose fundamental group is the binary icosahedral group,
\pi_{1}=2\mathsf{I}

Mermin 1979
.

Any non-trivial
\mathsf{I}
-irrep
\Gamma
“lifts” to an irrep of
2\mathsf{I}
by assigning the
-1

2\mathsf{I}
-group element to the kernel. This yields an irrep of
2\mathsf{I}
whose kernel is the double cover of that of
\Gamma
.
Equation (
156
) is satisfied,

\frac{\mathsf{I}}{\ker\Gamma}\cong\mathsf{I}\cong\frac{2\mathsf{I}}{2\ker\Gamma}~,

(160)

yielding a monodromy group that is non-trivial for any
\Gamma\neq\textsc{a}
.
In all cases, we have verified that the Berry connection is locally flat (see Table
6
), albeit mostly using numerics.
The other finite subgroups of
\mathsf{SO(3)}
follow similar behavior.

XI
Fiber codes

Following the notation of the previous sections, we define our encoding to be in the fiber degree of freedom of the “identity” orientation,
\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{e}
, of an entangled species
\Gamma_{\!\textnormal{rot}}\equiv\Gamma
(i.e., whose irrep dimension
\mathfrak{d}=\dim\Gamma>1
).
A basis of code states, or codewords, for this qu
\mathfrak{d}
it encoding consists of position states (
123
)

|\overline{\mu}\rangle\propto|\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{e},\mu\rangle\quad\quad\text{for}\quad\quad\mu\in\{1,2,\cdots,\mathfrak{d}\}~.

(161)

We omit the residual nuclear
|\chi\rangle
factor from now on since we do not use it to store information.

The above encoding lies fully in a
single
molecular orientation
\mathsf{e}
, and we will show that this encoding is comparable to other encodings for asymmetric molecules whose codewords are superpositions of
several
molecular orientations
Albert et al. 2020
.

Errors acting on the molecule can be grouped into two types — inter-species and intra-species.

Inter-species
errors cause the information to leak out of the
\Gamma
species into a different species of the same molecule.
Such errors can, in principle, be detected by monitoring the other species.
Some errors will also be correctable, but determining correctability requires developing a physical basis of inter-species operators — an open question from both the chemical and quantum-information perspectives that is outside the scope of this work (cf.
Jain et al. 2024
).

We focus on
intra-species
errors, which cause transitions within the given species.
We generalize the noise operators from Ref.
Albert et al. 2020
for asymmetric and rotationally
\mathsf{C}_{\infty}
-symmetric molecules to rotationally and perrotationally
\mathsf{G}
-symmetric species.

XI.1
Intra-species noise operators

Intra-species operators can shift the position of the molecule by applying some lab-frame rotation
\mathsf{r}
and/or kick the molecule’s angular momentum by some amount
J
.
General noise operators are products of position shifts
\overrightharpoon{X}
and momentum kicks
\hat{H}
,

\hat{E}_{m\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{r})={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{|\mathsf{G}|}}}~\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{r}}\hat{H}_{m\kappa}^{J}~.

(162)

Here,
J
,
m
, and
\kappa
are indices associated with the induced representation
\Gamma\uparrow\mathsf{SO(3)}
, and only kicks by these specified values keep the system within the given species.

Above, the
X
-type operators are lab-frame rotations acting as

\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{r}}|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathsf{G}|}}\sum_{\tau=1}^{\mathfrak{d}}s_{\tau}\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}\Gamma^{\mu\tau}(\mathsf{g})|\mathsf{r}\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}\rangle|\tau\rangle~,

(163a)

where, for convenience, we have expressed the codeword in its Zak form (
103
) in terms of
\mathsf{SO(3)}
position states
|\mathsf{r}\rangle
.
Here and for the remainder of this subsection, every ket that contains only
sans serif

text represents an
\mathsf{SO(3)}
position state.

The
Z
-type momentum kicks are diagonal in the position-state basis,

\hat{H}_{m\kappa}^{J}|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle=H_{m\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{a},\mu)|\mathsf{a},\mu\rangle~,

(163b)

whose diagonal matrix elements are
\mathsf{G}
-adapted harmonics (
116
).
One can show these form an orthonormal basis for functions on the induced representation using the
\mathsf{G}
-adapted orthogonality relation (
117
).

Products of shifts and kicks form a complete basis for the space of all physical operators on the species, meaning that any noise operator can be expanded as a sum of such products.
To show this, we need to prove the following,

\int_{\mathsf{SO(3)}}\textnormal{d}\mathsf{r}\sum_{J\downarrow\Gamma}\sum_{|m|\leq J}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{\mathfrak{m}(J)}\langle\mathsf{a},\mu|\hat{E}_{m\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{r})|\mathsf{b},\nu\rangle\langle\mathsf{b}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}|\hat{E}_{m\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{r})|\mathsf{a}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}\rangle^{\star}=\langle\mathsf{a},\mu|\mathsf{a}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}\rangle\langle\mathsf{b},\nu|\mathsf{b}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\rangle~,

(164)

for any coset representatives
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}^{\prime},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{b}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{SO(3)}/\mathsf{G}
and
\Gamma
-irrep elements
\mu,\mu^{\prime},\nu,\nu^{\prime}
.

Using the action (
163
) of the two types of noise operators, we first simplify the following matrix element to

\langle\mathsf{a},\mu|\hat{E}_{m\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{r})|\mathsf{b},\nu\rangle={\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{|\mathsf{G}|}}}H_{m\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{b},\nu)\sum_{\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{G}}\Gamma^{\nu\mu}(\mathsf{g})\langle\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}^{-1}|\mathsf{r}\mathsf{b}\rangle~.

(165)

Then, we plug this into two places in the left-hand side of Eq. (
164
) and apply the
\mathsf{G}
-adapted completeness relation (
117
), simplifying the left-hand side to

\!\!\!\!\langle\mathsf{b},\nu|\mathsf{b}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\rangle\!\!\int_{\mathsf{SO(3)}}\!\!\!\!\textnormal{d}\mathsf{r}{\textstyle\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{|\mathsf{G}|}}\sum_{\mathsf{g},\mathsf{h}\in\mathsf{G}}\Gamma^{\nu\mu}(\mathsf{g})\Gamma^{\nu\mu^{\prime}\star}(\mathsf{h})\langle\mathsf{a}\mathsf{g}^{-1}|\mathsf{r}\mathsf{b}\rangle\langle\mathsf{r}\mathsf{b}|\mathsf{a}^{\prime}\mathsf{h}^{-1}\rangle.

(166)

Finally, we notice that the outer product of position kets
|\mathsf{r}\mathsf{b}\rangle

yields the identity on
\mathsf{SO(3)}
position-state space when combined with the integral over
\mathsf{r}
.
Removing this identity, using Eq. (
78
), and applying Schur orthogonality over
\mathsf{G}
completes the proof.

XI.2
Protected encodings

A fiber code (
161
) detecting intra-species operators
\hat{E}
satisfies
Knill and Laflamme 1997

\langle\overline{\nu}|\hat{E}|\overline{\mu}\rangle=c_{\hat{E}}\delta_{\mu\nu}

(167)

for all codeword labels
\nu,\mu
.
In words, either the error causes the information to leave the code space (corresponding to
c_{\hat{E}}=0
) or the error acts as the identity on the codespace (up to the constant
c_{\hat{E}}
).
The constant can be infinite since we are using the idealized, non-normalizable position states as code states.
Using approximate but normalized position states will yield finite constants, and intrinsic memory errors (due to the approximate code states not being exactly orthogonal) should be suppressed with the energy of the states by the same reasoning as that from Sec.
X.4.3
.

We can utilize our holonomy calculations from Sec.
X
to show that all but a measure-zero set of position shifts are detectable.
Since all rotations
\overrightharpoon{X}_{\mathsf{r}}
, except for those in
\mathsf{G}
, map the codeword to a different coset, any rotations
not
in the symmetry group will map the codeword to an error state that is orthogonal to the original codespace.
In other words, intra-species noise operators satisfy

\langle\overline{\nu}|\hat{E}_{m\kappa}^{J}(\mathsf{r})|\overline{\mu}\rangle=0\quad\quad\forall\quad\mathsf{r}\notin\mathsf{G}\,,

(168)

detecting all rotations except those in the symmetry group (which in turn form fault-tolerant monodromy gates).
Moreover, rotations
\mathsf{r}\in\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{G}}}
(i.e., those that are also coset representatives closest to the identity) are correctable since one can undo them by rotating back to the codespace without inducing a monodromy.

Protecting against momentum kicks requires picking a species for which transitions between momentum states require more than a single quantum of angular momentum.
The set of momentum kicks
\hat{H}^{J}_{m\kappa}
for a given species
\Gamma
takes values in momenta
J\downarrow\Gamma
, i.e., those momenta which contain the species’ irrep when restricted to the symmetry group (see Sec.
VIII.2
).
This set has a minimal element
J_{\text{min}}
, and the corresponding species are immune to momentum kicks by
J<J_{\text{min}}

since kick operators by such amounts do not exist within the species.

We catalogue all vector irreps with
J_{\text{min}}>1
, which consist of all vector irreps in Table
6
excluding those that appear at
J=1
(namely, the tetrahedral
t
and the octahedral and icosahedral
\textsc{t}_{1}
irreps).
All such irreps yield fiber encodings that protect against both position shifts and momentum kicks and that require only a single molecular orientation.
The corresponding molecular species are marked with a filled-in star in the table.

Example 47

(
\mathsf{D}_{N}
symmetry)
.

Entangled molecular species with dihedral perrotational symmetry correspond to two-dimensional rotation irreps
\Gamma=\textsc{e}_{i}
for some
i\geq 1
.
Branching rules
Altmann and Herzig 1994
reveal that the
i
th irrep features first at angular momentum
i
, corresponding to
J_{\text{min}}=i
.
Fiber codes of such species are immune to momentum kicks up to
i-1
and encode a qubit (since
\mathfrak{d}=2
).
Such codes also correct rotations in
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{D}_{N}}}
.

The simplest case is
\mathsf{D}_{5}
symmetry, whose group has two 2D irreps,
\textsc{e}_{1}
and
\textsc{e}_{2}
.
The
\textsc{e}_{2}
species of such molecules is thus immune to single momentum kicks.
Perrotationally
\mathsf{D}_{5}
-symmetric molecules include pentagonal planar molecules like
\text{XeF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{5}}}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}^{\text{\hskip 0.90417pt--\hskip 0.90417pt}}
as well as the cyclopentadienide molecule and the rather large pentaphenyl-cyclopentadienyl molecule.

Dihedral fiber codes are comparable in performance to dihedral molecular codes, which are based on nested groups
\mathsf{D}_{N/2}\subset\mathsf{D}_{N}
(for even
N
)
Albert et al. 2020
.
Both codes encode a qubit and correct rotations in
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{D}_{N}}}
, but the number of detectable momentum kicks is never larger for fiber codes since the irrep index always satisfies
i<N/2
(with dihedral molecular codes detecting
<N/2
kicks).
However, fiber codes are much less “quantum”: dihedral molecular codewords consist of a superposition of
N/2
position states of any asymmetric molecule, while dihedral fiber codewords consist of a single position state of an entangled perrotationally
\mathsf{D}_{N}
-symmetric molecular species.
Moreover, the dihedral fiber code admits a fault-tolerant dihedral gate set via monodromy (see Table
6
), while the only fault-tolerant operation admitted by the molecular code is the logical-
X
codeword permutation.

Example 48

(
\mathsf{O}
symmetry)
.

The two-dimensional
e
and three-dimensional
\textsc{t}_{2}
irreps of the octahedral group both feature first at angular momentum
J=2
, meaning that fiber codes of either species are immune to single momentum kicks.
The codes encode a qubit and qutrit, respectively, and correct rotations in
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{O}}}
.
Examples include the
\textsc{e}^{\star}
and
\textsc{t}_{2}^{\star}
species of
\text{SF}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{6}}}
, respectively
Harter and Patterson 1977c
;
Harter and Patterson 1977a
;
McDowell et al. 1982
.

Octahedral fiber codes can be compared to qubit molecular codes based on nested subgroups
\mathsf{T}\subset\mathsf{O}
.
Both codes protect against rotations in
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{O}}}
, but the molecular code detects up to two momentum kicks, while either fiber code is immune to only a single kick.
The molecular code consists of superpositions of 12 molecular positions, while the fiber code encodes using only one.
Moreover, the octahedral fiber code admits either a fault-tolerant dihedral or octahedral gate set via monodromy (see Table
6
), while the only fault-tolerant operation admitted by the molecular code is the logical-
X
codeword permutation.

Example 49

(
\mathsf{I}
symmetry)
.

The five-dimensional
h
irrep of the icosahedral group features first at momentum
J=2
, meaning that its corresponding five-dimensional fiber encoding is immune to single momentum kicks.
At
J=3
, one encounters the three-dimensional
\textsc{t}_{2}
and four-dimensional
g
irreps, yielding respective qutrit and quartrit fiber codes immune to single and double momentum kicks.
All such codes can correct rotations in the Poincare sphere,
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{I}}}
.

Icosahedral fiber codes can be compared to molecular codes based on nested subgroups
\mathsf{T}\subset\mathsf{I}
, which encode a five-dimensional qudit.
Both codes protect against rotations in
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{SO(3)}}}{{\mathsf{I}}}
and admit a fault-tolerant implementation of the icosahedral gate group.
The molecular code detects up to two momentum kicks, one more than the
h
-irrep and the same as the
\textsc{t}_{2}
-irrep and
g
-irrep fiber codes.

Figure 10:

(a) Sketch of the matter-wave diffraction approach to induce a monodromy.
A single species of homonuclear diatomic (i.e., either para or ortho) is initialized in the span of states
|^{J}_{m=0}\rangle
and diffracted by a double slit.
Ultrafast laser pulses are applied to the lower arm (in the region outlined by the red square) to re-orient the molecules and induce a species-dependent monodromy of
\pm 1
(see Fig.
5
for details). The phase of the matter-wave interference fringes can be probed by monitoring the photo-ionization probability as a function of distance (red needle).
(b) In an alternative proposal, a set of oriented molecules is driven by a
\pi/2
-pulse into a superposition of ground (
v=0
) and excited (
v=1
) vibrational states.
The two states correspond to the two “arms” of an interferometer. The difference in rotational constants between (
v=0
) and (
v=1
) states makes one “arm” of the interferometer “dark” to the re-orienting pulse.

XII
Monodromy detection via interferometry

We turn to the problem of detecting the monodromy phase of homonuclear diatomic species.
One approach is molecular interferometry: conceptually, a cold beam of molecules of a particular symmetry species (e.g., ortho or para) is aligned in some fixed position state and then split into two “arms”. In practice, a fixed position state is difficult to realize, requiring a strong aligning field to be always present. We will comment on this further shortly.
A lab-based rotation, as described earlier, is applied to just one of the two arms.
Downstream, the two arms are then recombined, whereby they can interfere with each other.
In practice, the two arms can be separated in real space using matter wave diffraction, or by using the internal degrees of freedom of the molecule such as vibrational or electronic states.

As mentioned in Sec.
II.6
, initializing molecules in states other than position states can be used to simulate a monodromy.
Initializing in the span of
|^{J}_{m=0}\rangle
and applying an equatorial rotation also yields a
\pm 1
phase (see Example
39
), albeit a phase that can no longer be attributed to a monodromy in position-state space.

Matter-wave diffraction

Conceptually, the matter wave diffraction is easier to visualize, but in practice may yield poorer signal and phase contrast due to loss of molecular flux at each “beamsplitter” step of the interferometer.
In this approach, a beam of aligned molecules is incident on a double slit, whose two paths correspond to the arms of the interferometer [Fig.
10
(a)].

Next, the molecules in the lower arm are rotated according to the stroboscopic sequence of ultrafast pulses described in Fig.
5
.
Hitting the molecule with an ultrafast pulse at every rotational revival time (
T_{\text{rev}}=2\pi/B\sim 10
ps, with
B
the rotational constant) is experimentally challenging, as typical pulsed laser repetition rates are too low.
Furthermore, realistic molecules can support only about 10 good rotational revivals prior to dephasing due to rovibrational coupling
Dooley et al. 2003
;
Spanner 2004
;
Lee et al. 2006
, so one cannot apply realigning pulses at arbitrarily large integer multiples of
T_{\text{rev}}
.

One solution is to split up the aligning laser beam path into
N
arms, with the
j
th arm experiencing a relative path length delay of
jT_{\text{rev}}
, before recombining the arms again and directing them onto the molecules.
Available laser power severely limits the number of times we can split and recombine a beam, meaning that we can afford roughly
N\sim 3
arms.

The polarization in the
j
th arm would then need to be tilted by
\pi/3\sim 60
degrees relative to the
(j+1)
-st arm.
As long as the tilt is less than
90
degrees, there should be no ambiguity as to which direction the molecule should rotate.
Within the three revivals, a typical diatomic molecule in a supersonic molecular beam (longitudinal speed
500
m/s) only travels
\sim 100
nm, allowing it to be fully reoriented well within the
10

\mu
m waist of a single aligning beam.

Further downstream, those rotated molecules are recombined with the un-rotated molecules in the upper arm due to diffraction.
The matter-wave interference leads to fringes in the molecular flux.
The fringes are phase shifted compared to those obtained in a classic double slit experiment (i.e., when no molecular rotation is induced in the lower arm).
The phase of the molecular interference fringes could be observed by scanning an ionization laser vertically and recording molecular ion counts as a function of distance.

Internal state interferometry

Another method is to use the molecular internal degrees of freedom for interferometry. Instead of a double slit, a
\pi/2
-pulse resonant with a transition to the first excited
v
ibrational (or metastable electronic) state is applied.
This generates a coherent superposition of aligned molecules in vibrational states
v=0
and
v=1
.
An aligning pulse with a repetition frequency equal to the first rotational spacing in the ground state
v=0
will be able to excite resonant two-photon transitions, thereby mixing states of neighboring momenta.
Generally, however, the rotational constant in the
v=1
state will be different, and this would make its rotational states off resonant (and therefore “invisible”) with respect to the aligning pulse [see Fig.
10
(b)].
This requires choosing a molecule whose rotational constant at
v=1
is sufficiently incommensurate with that of the ground state so that only the ground-state component is affected by the aligning pulses within the excitation bandwidth.

Assuming the excited state is dark to the alignment pulses, only the ground-state component of the vibrational superposition is rotated in the lab frame.
The two components can be subsequently recombined with a second
\pi/2
-pulse further downstream.
Population in
v=0
or
v=1
can be measured via resonant photo ionization, and should be modulated depending on whether the re-orientation pulses are active or not.
The advantage over matter-wave diffraction is that more of the molecular beam flux can be preserved at every “beamsplitter” step.

We now return to the issue of
preparing the incident molecular beam.
We have assumed that all molecules remain aligned in the lab frame unless actively rotated, but in reality such alignment can be only transient.
Furthermore, aligned molecules are in (approximate) position states, which are superpositions of many rotational states.
It remains to be checked whether the condition of incommensurability (i.e., off-resonant aligning pulses) in the vibrational-state interferometer can be met if the molecules already populate many rotational states to begin with.
